Implicit Linear Algebra and Basic Circuit Theory II: port behaviour of rigid multiports Journal: Linear Algebra and its Applications
H. Narayanan

Abstract
In this paper, we define the notion of rigidity for linear electrical multiports and for matroid pairs. We show the parallel between the two and study the consequences of this parallel. We present applications to testing, using purely matroidal methods, whether a connection of rigid multiports yields a linear network with unique solution. We also indicate that rigidity can be regarded as the closest notion to duality that can be hoped for, when the spaces correspond to different physical constraints, such as topological and device characteristic. A vector space 𝒱 S {\cal V}_{S} is a collection of row vectors, over a field 𝔽 \mathbb{F} on a finite column set S , S, closed under addition and multiplication by elements of 𝔽 . \mathbb{F}. We denote the space of all vectors on S S by ℱ S \mbox{$\cal F$}_{S} and the space containing only the zero vector on S S by 𝟎 S . {\mathbf{0}}_{S}. An affine space 𝒜 S {\cal A}_{S} is the sum of a vector α S \alpha_{S} with 𝒱 S . {\cal V}_{S}. The dual 𝒱 S ⟂ {\cal V}_{S}^{\perp} of a vector space 𝒱 S {\cal V}_{S} is the collection of all vectors whose dot product with vectors in 𝒱 S \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S} is zero. A matroid ℳ S \mbox{$\cal M$}_{S} on S , S, is a family of ‘independent’ sets with the property that maximal independent sets contained in any given subset of S S have the same cardinality. A multiport is an ordered pair ( 𝒱 A ​ B 1 , 𝒜 B 2 ) , (\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB},{\cal A}^{2}_{B}), where 𝒱 A ​ B 1 \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB} is the solution space on A ⊎ B A\uplus B of the Kirchhoff current and voltage equations of the graph of the multiport and 𝒜 B 2 : ≡ α B + 𝒱 B 2 {\cal A}^{2}_{B}:\equiv\alpha_{B}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B} is the device characteristic of the multiport, with A A corresponding to port voltages and currents and B B corresponding to internal voltages and currents. A solution of ( 𝒱 A ​ B 1 , 𝒜 B 2 ) , (\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB},{\cal A}^{2}_{B}), is a vector ( x A , x B ) (x_{A},x_{B}) such that ( x A , x B ) ∈ 𝒱 A ​ B 1 , x B ∈ 𝒜 B 2 . (x_{A},x_{B})\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB},x_{B}\in{\cal A}^{2}_{B}. A network is a multiport ( 𝒱 B 1 , 𝒜 B 2 ) . (\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},{\cal A}^{2}_{B}). The pair { 𝒱 A ​ B 1 , α B + 𝒱 B 2 } \{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB},\alpha_{B}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\} is said to be rigid iff it has a solution ( x A , x B ) (x_{A},x_{B}) for every vector α B \alpha_{B} and given a restriction x A x_{A} of the solution, x B x_{B} is unique. A rigid network has a unique solution. The pair { ℳ A ​ B 1 , ℳ B 2 } \{\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\} is said to be rigid iff the two matroids have disjoint bases which cover B . B. We show that the properties of rigid pairs of matroids closely parallel those of rigid multiports. New multiports can be constructed from multiports ( 𝒱 A ​ B 1 , 𝒜 B 2 ) , ( 𝒱 C ​ D 1 , 𝒜 D 2 ) , (\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB},{\cal A}^{2}_{B}),(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{CD},{\cal A}^{2}_{D}), A , B , C , D , A,B,C,D, pairwise disjoint, by forcing the variables on A 1 ⊆ A A_{1}\subseteq A and D 1 ⊆ D D_{1}\subseteq D to lie in an affine space 𝒜 A 1 ​ C 1 . {\cal A}_{A_{1}C_{1}}. This new multiport ( ( 𝒱 A ​ B 1 ⊕ 𝒱 C ​ D 1 ) , ( 𝒜 B 2 ⊕ 𝒜 D 2 ⊕ 𝒜 A 1 ​ C 1 ) ) ((\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB}\oplus\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{CD}),({\cal A}^{2}_{B}\oplus{\cal A}^{2}_{D}\oplus{\cal A}_{A_{1}C_{1}})) is said to be obtained from the original ones by connection through 𝒜 A 1 ​ C 1 . {\cal A}_{A_{1}C_{1}}. We give necessary and sufficient conditions for this multiport to be rigid given that the original multiports are rigid. Networks are usually constructed in this manner from smaller multiports, so that these conditions are necessary and sufficient for the final network to have a unique solution. We use the methods developed in the paper to show that a multiport with independent and controlled sources and positive or negative resistors, whose parameters can be taken to be algebraically independent over ℚ , {\mathbb{Q}}, is rigid, if certain simple topological conditions are satisfied by the device edges.

1.2
Notational changes from ILABCI

‘Regular multiports’ are now replaced by ‘Rigid Multiports’.
This was felt necessary since ‘regular’
is already overused in the literature.
Further, a network with unique solution is analogous to
rigid structures in statics.
The methods of this paper are equally applicable to static structures.

2
Introduction

The notion of multiports is fundamental to, and characteristic of, electrical
network theory
[
1
,
2
]
. Decomposition into multiports is a useful technique
for theoretical and computational analysis of the network
[
19
]
.
It is also the natural way of synthesizing a complex network in terms of
simpler ones
[
1
]
. In recent times the importance of ports has also been emphasized
for system theory
[
37
]
.

An electrical network can be regarded as a theoretical
model of a practical circuit. A test for validity of this model
is whether it has a unique solution. A natural question is, ‘what is the
corresponding test for validity of a multiport as a model for a practical
multiport?’. We claim that the test should be for ‘rigidity’ of the multiport
when it is linear. This paper is an attempt to justify this claim.

We say that a linear multiport is rigid if it has a solution for arbitrary
source values and has a unique solution for a given valid port condition.
To see the relevance of the notion, we note that
when the multiport is a network without ports, rigidity translates to the network having a unique solution.
Further, if multiports are to be connected to form a network with unique solution, it is necessary that they be rigid.
Therefore, it is only rigid multiports which, after some preprocessing,
can be handled by conventional circuit simulators.
The notion is seen to be natural, when we show in this paper that
if we connect rigid multiports, to retain rigidity, we only have to
verify certain interface properties, which again can be interpreted in terms
of rigidity.

Rigid multiports behave the way linear multiports are expected to behave
by a working engineer.
For instance, we show in this paper, if the device characteristic is ‘proper’ (dimension equal to half
the number of current and voltage variables), the multiport behaviour (the affine space of voltage current pairs that can occur at the ports) will be proper.
Further, under simple topological conditions, assuming algebraic independence
of parameters of the device characteristic, the port behaviour will actually
be ‘hybrid’ (in the equations of the port behaviour, exactly one of the current-voltage variables of a port will occur on the left side).

We show that there is a corresponding notion of rigidity for pairs of matroids and its properties
exactly mimic those of multiport rigidity. We exploit this similarity between
multiports and matroid pairs to derive matroidal tests for rigidity
of multiports which are made up of nonzero resistors, independent and controlled sources,
assuming that the resistances, gains, transresistances, transconductances, are algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}}.

We now give a brief summary of the paper.

A vector space
{\cal V}_{S}
is a collection of row vectors, over a field
\mathbb{F}
on a finite column set
S,
closed under addition and multiplication by elements
of
\mathbb{F}.

(The vector space may be thought of as composed of row vectors and
S
may be regarded as the column set.)
A linear multiport can be treated as an ordered pair
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB},{\cal A}^{2}_{B}),

where
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{AB}
is the solution space on
A\uplus B
of the Kirchhoff current and voltage
equations of the graph of the multiport and
{\cal A}^{2}_{B}:\equiv\alpha_{B}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}
is
the device characteristic of the multiport, with
A
corresponding to port voltages
and currents and
B
corresponding to internal voltages and currents.
(We refer to
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}
as the vector space translate of
{\cal A}_{B}.
)
A solution of the multiport is a vector of the form

(v_{P^{\prime}},i_{P"},v_{S^{\prime}},i_{S"}),
with
P^{\prime},P",
being disjoint copies of the port branches
P
and
S^{\prime},S",
being disjoint copies of the internal
branches
S,

A:\equiv P^{\prime}\uplus P"
and
B:\equiv S^{\prime}\uplus S".

This vector is a solution of the multiport provided it belongs to
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}

and its restriction
(v_{S^{\prime}},i_{S"}),
belongs to
{\cal A}_{B}.

We say that the linear multiport is rigid iff

(a) it has a solution for arbitrary values of sources, i.e., for arbitrary
values of
\alpha_{B},

and

(b) for a given valid port condition (a vector
(v_{P^{\prime}},i_{P"})
that is a restriction of a solution of the multiport)
there is a unique multiport solution.

If either of the above conditions is violated, freely available
circuit simulators (such as SPICE) cannot handle networks made up of the multiports.

Rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},{\cal A}_{B}\},
can be shown to be equivalent to the
condition

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}+{\mathbf{0}}_{A}))=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}+(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}^{\perp}+{\mathbf{0}}_{A}))=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}^{\perp}).

(
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}^{\perp}
is the collection of all vectors on
X
whose dot product
with every vector in
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
is zero.)

Multiports are usually connected together to form larger multiports,
through additional device characteristic at some of the ports.
So the natural question is, under what conditions is such a connection
of rigid multiports, rigid.
To answer this and a related question in brief, we introduce some notation.

For any vector space
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},
where
A,B
are disjoint, we define

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-A)B}:\equiv\{(-f_{A},f_{B}):(f_{A},f_{B})\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\}.

Let
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal V}_{PQ}
be vector spaces on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,
where
S,P,Q

are pairwise disjoint.
We denote the space of all vectors on
S
by
\mbox{$\cal F$}_{S}

and the space containing only the zero vector on
S
by
{\mathbf{0}}_{S}.

We define the
restriction of
{\mathcal{V}_{SP}}
to
S
to be

{\mathcal{V}_{SP}\circ S}:\equiv\{f_{S}:(f_{S},f_{P})\in\mathcal{V}_{SP}\},

the contraction of
{\mathcal{V}_{SP}}
to
S
to be

{\mathcal{V}_{SP}\times S}:\equiv\{f_{S}:(f_{S},0_{P})\in\mathcal{V}_{SP}\},

a minor of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
to be a vector space
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\circ X_{1})\times X_{2},X_{2}\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq X.
We define the sum

\mathcal{V}_{SP}+\mathcal{V}_{PQ}
to be
(\mathcal{V}_{SP}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{Q})+({\mathbf{0}}_{S}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{PQ}),
the intersection

{\mathcal{V}_{SP}\cap\mathcal{V}_{PQ}}
of
\mathcal{V}_{SP}
,
\mathcal{V}_{PQ}
to be

(\mathcal{V}_{SP}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{Q})\cap(\mbox{$\cal F$}_{S}\oplus\mathcal{V}_{PQ}),

the matched composition

{\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{PQ}
to be
({\cal V}_{SP}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q})\times(S\uplus Q)

and the skewed composition

{\cal V}_{SP}\rightleftharpoons{\cal V}_{PQ}
to be
({\cal V}_{SP}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{PQ})\times(S\uplus Q).

The implicit duality theorem for vector spaces (Theorem
11
)
states that
({\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{PQ})^{\perp}\ =\ {\cal V}_{SP}^{\perp}\rightleftharpoons{\cal V}_{PQ}^{\perp}.

In terms of the above notation,
we have two questions:

Question 1
. Let
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{ABC},{\cal A}_{C})
be a rigid multiport, where
A\uplus B
correspond to port variables and
C,
to internal variables. Suppose we impose
additional device characteristic
{\cal A}_{B}
on the port variables
B.

When would
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{ABC},{\cal A}_{B}\oplus{\cal A}_{C})
be rigid? The answer is ‘it is rigid iff
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{ABC}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{C},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})
is rigid’ (Theorem
24
),

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{C},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B},
being vector space translates of
{\cal A}_{C},{\cal A}_{B},
respectively.

Question 2
.
‘given two rigid multiports
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{ABC},{\cal A}_{C}),(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{D\tilde{B}E},{\cal A}_{E}),

A,B,C,D,\tilde{B},E,
being pairwise disjoint, when is the connection

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{ABC}\oplus\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{D\tilde{B}E},{\cal A}_{C}\oplus{\cal A}_{E}\oplus{\cal A}_{B\tilde{B}}),

rigid?’ and the answer is ‘it is rigid iff
([(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{ABC}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{C})\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{D\tilde{B}E}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{E})],\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B\tilde{B}})
is rigid’ (Theorem
25
).

Next, we show that there is an analogous notion of rigidity for pairs of matroids which leads to a parallel theory to that of pairs of vector spaces.

A matroid
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}
on
S,

is a family of ‘independent’ sets with the property that maximal
independent sets contained in any given subset of
S
have the same cardinality.
A base of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}
is a maximal independent subset of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}
contained in
S.

The rank
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S})
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}
is the cardinality of any of its bases.
The dual
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}^{*}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}
is the matroid whose bases are complements
of bases of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}.
We denote the matroid on
S
for which
S
is independent, by

\mbox{$\cal F$}_{S}

and the matroid on
S
which has no independent sets, by

{\mathbf{0}}_{S}.

Let
{\cal M}_{SP},{\cal M}_{PQ}
be matroids on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,
where
S,P,Q

are pairwise disjoint.
We define the
restriction of
{\mathcal{M}_{SP}}
to
S
to be
the matroid on
S,
whose independent sets are the same as those of
{\mathcal{M}_{SP}}
that are contained in
S
,
the contraction of
{\mathcal{M}_{SP}}
to
S
to be

{\mathcal{M}_{SP}\times S}:\equiv(\mathcal{M}^{*}_{SP}\circ S)^{*},

a minor of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X}
to be a matroid
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X}\circ X_{1})\times X_{2},X_{2}\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq X.
We define the
union
\mathcal{M}_{SP}\vee\mathcal{M}_{PQ}
to be the matroid on
S\uplus P\uplus Q,

whose independent sets are unions of independent sets of the individual
matroids
\mathcal{M}_{SP}
and
\mathcal{M}_{PQ}

and the matched composition

{\cal M}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal M}_{PQ}
to be
({\cal M}_{SP}\vee{\cal M}_{PQ})\times(S\uplus Q).

The implicit duality theorem for matroids (Theorem
50
)
states that
({\cal M}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal M}_{PQ})^{*}={\cal M}_{SP}^{*}\leftrightarrow{\cal M}_{PQ}^{*}.

We say the pair
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B}\}
is rigid iff

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B})\ \ \mbox{and}\ \ r((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})^{*}\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B})^{*})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B}^{*}).

There is a natural way of associating a matroid with a vector space.
The matroid
\mbox{$\cal M$}({\cal V}_{S})
is defined to be the matroid on
S
whose
independent sets are the independent columns of
{\cal V}_{S}.

It can be shown that
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})\}
is rigid when
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\}
is rigid (Theorem
56
).

Analogous to the answers to the two questions on rigidity of vector spaces,
we have that

when
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{ABC},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{C})
is given to be rigid,
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{ABC},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{C})
is rigid iff
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{ABC}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{C},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B})
is rigid
and that
when
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{ABC},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{C}),(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{D\tilde{B}E},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{E})
are given to be rigid,

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{ABC}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{D\tilde{B}E},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{C}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{E}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B\tilde{B}})

is rigid
iff
([(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{ABC}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{C})\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{D\tilde{B}E}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{E})],\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B\tilde{B}})
is rigid.
These follow from Theorem
55
.

The link between the results on vector space pair rigidity and matroid pair rigidity
arises when we have
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})
and

\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}^{\perp})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}^{\perp}).

This happens, for instance, when the device characteristic
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}
of the multiport
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})
is characterized by parameters
which are algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}},

with the vector space
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}
being over
{\mathbb{Q}}.

For such pairs
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\},
we have that

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})\}
is rigid iff
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\}
is rigid
and that
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})

=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})
(Theorem
56
).
Further when
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}
are minors of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AX},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},
respectively
and satisfy certain additional conditions, we can show that rigidity of the former pair is equivalent to the rigidity of the latter pair.
There is a parallel result for matroid pairs where we can show that
when
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B}
are minors of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AX},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X},
respectively
and satisfy certain additional conditions, the rigidity of the former pair is equivalent to the rigidity of the latter pair.
Because of these results, starting with the fact that
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})\}
is rigid iff
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\}
is rigid
we can conclude that
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AX}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X})\}
is rigid iff
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AX},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\}
is rigid (Theorem
72
).

We use these ideas
to show that a multiport
with independent and controlled sources and positive or negative resistors, whose parameters can be taken to be algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}},

is rigid if

(a) the voltage sources, controlled as well as independent,
do not form a loop with controlling current branches and
(b) the current sources, controlled as well as independent,
do not form a cutset with controlling voltage branches (Theorem
68
). In this case the test for rigidity is linear time on the
size of the graph of the multiport and additionally the multiport
behaviour
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AX}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
has a hybrid representation and can be built in a convenient way.

The work reported here has its roots in the work of Purslow,
who gave necessary conditions for a circuit with resistors and
controlled sources to have a unique solution
[
28
]
,
and Iri and Tomizawa, who introduced the approach using matroids
exploiting algebraic
independence of parameters
[
5
,
6
]
.
Extensive work of this kind was carried out in the 70’s and 80’s studying the solvability of
systems
(specifically using the matroid union and matroid intersection theorems

[
3
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
,
9
,
10
,
11
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
29
,
30
]
).
A summary of the work relevant to uniqueness of solution for linear networks according to this approach is available
in
[
31
]
. In our case, the building blocks are rigid multiports and we need rigidity to be preserved even after connection
of these building blocks. This requires some additional techniques,
such as the use of the implicit duality theorem for vector spaces
and matroids (Theorems
11
,
50
).

We now present a brief outline of the paper.

Section
3
is on vector space and network preliminaries.
Among other things, it contains an extended definition of sum and intersection
of (row) vector spaces with different column sets, the definition
of matched and skewed composition and basic
definitions on multiports.

Section
4
is on two basic theorems of ‘implicit linear algebra’

[
25
]
- implicit inversion theorem, which is used to discuss
‘port transformation’ (
D
) and implicit duality theorem which is used extensively in the paper.

Section
5
is on rigid multiports which are studied in terms of
the properties of rigid pairs of affine spaces.
Theorem
17
contains the basic properties. The main theorem
from the point of view of applications is Theorem
21
, which states that
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{V})
is rigid iff

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{V})
and
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{WTV}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{V},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{T})
are rigid.
Subsection
5.4
generalizes these ideas to
families of affine spaces on sets which have the property that no element
belongs to more than two of the underlying sets. Theorem
38
is
the general version of Theorem
21
.
It reduces the rigidity of a family of affine spaces of the above kind, recursively, to
rigidity of two complementary subfamilies and an interface pair of affine spaces.
Theorem
41
is a version which is in terms of
a partition of the original family and a corresponding interface
set of affine spaces.

Section
6
is on matroidal rigidity. It begins with
a subsection on matroid preliminaries which is deliberately written in a
manner parallel to vector space preliminaries in Section
3
. This is followed by a subsection on rigidity of matroid pairs, which
parallels the development of rigid pairs of affine spaces of Section
5
. The main theorem
from the point of view of applications is Theorem
55
,
which states that
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})
is rigid iff

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})
and
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})
are rigid.
The proof of this theorem is a line by line ‘matroidal’ translation of that of the corresponding vector space result (Theorem
21
). It is relegated to

A
.
Subsection
6.6
generalizes these ideas to
families of affine spaces on sets which have the property that no element
belongs to more than two of the underlying sets. Theorem
65
is
the general version of Theorem
55
.
It reduces the rigidity of a family of matroids of the above kind, recursively, to
rigidity of subfamilies and an interface pair of matroids.
Theorem
67
is a version which is in terms of
a partition of the original family and a corresponding interface
set of matroids.

Section
7
is on connecting rigid multiports to yield a
rigid multiport. The main theme is on matroidal tests for rigidity of multiports. This section uses the ideas in Sections
5
and
6

to give a simple topological sufficient condition for rigidity of multiports containing
non zero resistors and independent and controlled sources (Theorem
68
). The proof requires the development of a theory of the relation between
multiport and matroid pair rigidity. This development is given in
B
.
Theorem
69
is a generalization of Theorem
68
that includes gyrators and ideal transformers in the set of devices.
This requires the testing of independence of certain sets of columns of a Dirac
structure. The proof is given in
C
.
The Dirac structure itself arises as the port behaviour of a multiport
which has only devices with a Dirac structure.
It is more convenient to work with a corresponding multiport with the same Dirac devices but with
minimal ports. This requires the idea of port minimization which is
discussed in
D
.

Section
8
is on conclusions.

3
Preliminaries

The preliminary results and the notation used are from
[
19
]
.

A
vector

\boldsymbol{f}
on a finite set
X
over
\mathbb{F}
is a function
f:X\rightarrow\mathbb{F}
where
\mathbb{F}
is a field.

The
sets
on which vectors are defined are always
finite
. When a vector
x
figures in an equation, we use the
convention that
x
denotes a column vector and
x^{T}
denotes a row vector such as
in ‘
Ax=b,x^{T}A=b^{T}
’. Let
f_{Y}
be a vector on
Y
and let
X\subseteq Y
. The
restriction
f_{Y}|_{X}
of
f_{Y}
to
X
is defined as follows:

f_{Y}|_{X}:\equiv g_{X},\textrm{ where }g_{X}(e)=f_{Y}(e),e\in X.

When
f
is on
X
over
\mathbb{F}
,
\lambda\in\mathbb{F},
the
scalar multiplication

\boldsymbol{\lambda f}
of
f
is on
X
and is defined by
(\lambda f)(e):\equiv\lambda[f(e)]
,
e\in X
. When
f
is on
X
and
g
on
Y
and both are over
\mathbb{F}
, we define
\boldsymbol{f+g}
on
X\cup Y
by

(f+g)(e):\equiv f(e)+g(e),e\in X\cap Y,\ (f+g)(e):\equiv f(e),e\in X\setminus Y,\ (f+g)(e):\equiv g(e),e\in Y\setminus X.

(For ease in readability, we will henceforth use
X-Y
in place of
X\setminus Y.
)

When
X,Y,
are disjoint,
f_{X}+g_{Y}
is written as
\boldsymbol{(f_{X},g_{Y})}.
When
f,g
are on
X
over
\mathbb{F},
the
dot product

\langle f,g\rangle
of
f
and
g
is defined by

\langle f,g\rangle:\equiv\sum_{e\in X}f(e)g(e).

When
X
,
Y
are disjoint,
\boldsymbol{X\uplus Y}
denotes the disjoint
union of
X
and
Y.
A vector
f_{X\uplus Y}
on
X\uplus Y
is written as
\boldsymbol{f_{XY}}.

We say
f
,
g
are
orthogonal
(orthogonal) iff
\langle f,g\rangle
is zero.

An
arbitrary collection
of vectors on
X

is denoted by
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{X}}
.
When
X
,
Y
are disjoint we usually write
\mathcal{K}_{XY}
in place of
\mathcal{K}_{X\uplus Y}
.
We write
{\cal K}_{XY}
as
{\cal K}_{X}\oplus{\cal K}_{Y}
iff

{\cal K}_{XY}:\equiv\{f_{XY}:f_{XY}=(f_{X},g_{Y}),f_{X}\in{\cal K}_{X},g_{Y}\in{\cal K}_{Y}\}.

We refer to
{\cal K}_{X}\oplus{\cal K}_{Y}
as the
direct sum
of
{\cal K}_{X},{\cal K}_{Y}.

A collection
{\cal K}_{X}
is a
vector space
on
X
iff it is closed under
addition and scalar multiplication.
The notation
\boldsymbol{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}}
always denotes
a vector space on
X.

For any collection
{\cal K}_{X},

\boldsymbol{span({\cal K}_{X})}
is the vector space of all
linear combinations of vectors in it.
We say
{\cal K}_{X}
is an
affine space
on
X,
iff it can be expressed as

x_{X}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},
where
x_{X}
is a vector and
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},
a vector space on
X.

The latter is unique for the affine space
{\cal K}_{X}
and is said to be its
vector space translate
.
The notation
\boldsymbol{{\cal A}_{X}}
always denotes
an
affine space
on
X.

When
{\cal A}_{X}
and
{\cal A}^{\prime}_{X}
have the same vector space translate, we say that
they are
translates
of each other.

For a vector space
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},
since we take
X
to be finite,
any maximal independent subset of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
has size less than or equal to
|X|
and this
size can be shown
to be unique. A maximal independent subset of a vector
space
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
is called its
basis
and its size
is called the
dimension
or
rank
of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
and denoted by
{\boldsymbol{dim}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X})}

or by
{\boldsymbol{r}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X})}.

For any collection of vectors
{\cal K}_{X},

the rank
\boldsymbol{r}({\cal K}_{X})

is defined to be
dim(span({\cal K}_{X})).

The collection of all linear combinations of the rows of a matrix
A
is a vector space
that is denoted by
row(A).

For any collection of vectors

\mathcal{K}_{X},
the collection
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{X}^{\perp}}
is defined by

{\mathcal{K}_{X}^{\perp}}:\equiv\{g_{X}:\langle f_{X},g_{X}\rangle=0\},

It is clear that
\mathcal{K}_{X}^{\perp}
is a vector space for
any
\mathcal{K}_{X}.
When
\mathcal{K}_{X}
is a vector space
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},

and the underlying set
X
is finite, it can be shown that
({\mathcal{V}_{X}^{\perp}})^{\perp}=\mathcal{V}_{X}

and
\mathcal{V}_{X},{\mathcal{V}_{X}^{\perp}}
are said to be
complementary orthogonal
.
The symbol
0_{X}
refers to the
zero vector
on
X
and
\boldsymbol{0_{X}}
refers to the
zero vector space
on
X.
The symbol
\boldsymbol{\mbox{$\cal F$}_{X}}
refers to the collection of all vectors on
X
over the field in question.
It is easily seen, when
X,Y
are disjoint, and
{\cal K}_{X},{\cal K}_{Y}

contain zero vectors, that
({\cal K}_{X}\oplus{\cal K}_{Y})^{\perp}={\cal K}_{X}^{\perp}\oplus{\cal K}_{Y}^{\perp}.

A matrix of full row rank, whose rows generate a vector space
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},

is called a
representative matrix
for
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}.

We will refer to the index set
X
as the
set of columns
of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}.

If
B^{1}_{X},B^{2}_{X}
are representative matrices of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},
it is clear
that the solution spaces of
B^{1}_{X}y=0
and
B^{2}_{X}y=0
are identical, being equal to
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{X}.
A consequence is that a subset of columns of
B^{1}_{X}
is independent iff it is independent in
B^{2}_{X}.

We say, in such a case, that the corresponding columns of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
are independent.
A
column base of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
is a maximally independent set of columns of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}.
A
column cobase of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
is the complement
X-T
of a column base
T

of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}.

A representative matrix which can be put in the form
(I\ |\ K)
after column
permutation, is called a
standard representative matrix
.
It is clear that every vector space has a standard representative matrix.
If
(I\ |\ K)
is a standard representative matrix of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},

it is easy to see that
(-K^{T}|I)
is a standard representative matrix of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{X}.

Therefore we have the following result.

Theorem 1
.

Let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
be a vector space on
X.
Then

1.

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{X})=|X|
and
((\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X})^{\perp})^{\perp}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X};

2.

T
is a column base of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
iff
T
is a column cobase of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{X}.

Remark 1
.

When the field
\mathbb{F}:\equiv\mathbb{C},
it is usual to interpret
the dot product
\langle f_{X},g_{X}\rangle
of
f_{X},g_{X}
to be
the inner product
\Sigma f(e)\overline{g(e)},e\in X,

where
\overline{g(e)}
is the complex conjugate of
g(e).

In place of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{X}
we must use
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{*}_{X},

where
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{*}_{X}:\equiv\{g_{X}:\langle f_{X},g_{X}\rangle=0\},

\langle f_{X},g_{X}\rangle
being taken to be as above.
The definition of adjoint, which is introduced later, must be in terms of

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{*}_{X}
instead of in terms of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{X}.

In this case, in place of the transpose of a matrix
Z
we use
Z^{*},
the conjugate
transpose of
Z.

The above interpretation of dot product is essential if we wish to
define the notion of power for networks defined over the complex field.

The
Implicit Duality Theorem (Theorem
11
), would go through
with either definition of dot product and the corresponding
definition of orthogonality.

The collection

\{(f_{X},\lambda f_{Y}):(f_{X},f_{Y})\in\mathcal{K}_{XY}\}

is denoted by

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{X(\lambda Y)}}.

When
\lambda=-1
we would write
{\mathcal{K}_{X(\lambda Y)}}
more simply as
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{X(-Y)}}.

Observe that
(\mathcal{K}_{X(-Y)})_{X(-Y)}=\mathcal{K}_{XY}.

We say sets
X
,
X^{\prime}
are
copies of each other
iff they are disjoint and there is a bijection, usually clear from the context, mapping
e\in X
to
e^{\prime}\in X^{\prime}
.
When
X,X^{\prime}
are copies of each other, the vectors
f_{X}
and
f_{X^{\prime}}
are said to be copies of each other with
f_{X^{\prime}}(e^{\prime}):\equiv f_{X}(e),e\in X.

The copy
({\cal K}_{X})_{X^{\prime}}
of
{\cal K}_{X}
is defined by

({\cal K}_{X})_{X^{\prime}}:\equiv\{f_{X^{\prime}}:f_{X}\in{\cal K}_{X}\}.

When
X
and
X^{\prime}
are copies of each other, the notation for interchanging the positions of variables with index sets
X
and
X^{\prime}
in a collection
\mathcal{K}_{XX^{\prime}Y}
is given by
\boldsymbol{(\mathcal{K}_{XX^{\prime}Y})_{X^{\prime}XY}}
, that is

(\mathcal{K}_{XX^{\prime}Y})_{X^{\prime}XY}:\equiv\{(g_{X},f_{X^{\prime}},h_{Y})\ :\ (f_{X},g_{X^{\prime}},h_{Y})\in\mathcal{K}_{XX^{\prime}Y},\ g_{X}\textrm{ being copy of }g_{X^{\prime}},\ f_{X^{\prime}}\textrm{ being copy of }f_{X}\}.

An affine space
\mathcal{A}_{XX^{\prime}}
is said to be
proper

iff the rank of its vector space translate is
|X|=|X^{\prime}|.

3.1
Restriction and contraction

The
restriction
of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SP}}
to
S
is defined by

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SP}\circ S}:\equiv\{f_{S}:(f_{S},f_{P})\in\mathcal{K}_{SP}\}.

The
contraction
of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SP}}
to
S
is defined by

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SP}\times S}:\equiv\{f_{S}:(f_{S},0_{P})\in\mathcal{K}_{SP}\}.

The sets on which we perform the contraction operation would always
have the zero vector as a member so that the resulting set would be nonvoid.
It is clear that restriction and contraction of vector spaces are also
vector spaces.
Here again,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SPZ}\circ SP}
,
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SPZ}\times SP}
, respectively
when
S,P,Z,
are pairwise disjoint, denote
{\mathcal{K}_{S\uplus P\uplus Z}\circ(S\uplus P)}
,
{\mathcal{K}_{S\uplus P\uplus Z}\times(S\uplus P)}.

We usually write
({\cal K}_{S}\circ A)\times B,({\cal K}_{S}\times A)\circ B
respectively, more simply as
{\cal K}_{S}\circ A\times B,{\cal K}_{S}\times A\circ B.

We refer to
{\cal K}_{S}\circ A\times B,B\subseteq A\subseteq S,
as a
minor
of
{\cal K}_{S}.

We have the following set of basic results regarding minors and complementary orthogonal vector spaces.

Theorem 2
.

Let
S\cap P=\emptyset,T_{2}\subseteq T_{1}\subseteq S,

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S}
be a vector space on
S,

{\cal V}_{SP}
be a vector space on
S\uplus P.

We then have the following.

1.

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S}\times T_{1})\cdot T_{2}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S}\cdot(S-(T_{1}-T_{2}))\times T_{2}.

2.

r({\cal V}_{SP})=r({\cal V}_{SP}\circ S)+r({\cal V}_{SP}\times P).

3.

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{SP}^{\perp}\circ P=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{SP}\times P)^{\perp}.

4.

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{SP}^{\perp}\times S=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{SP}\circ S)^{\perp}.

5.

P_{1}\subseteq P
is a column base of
{\cal V}_{SP}\times P
iff
there exists
S_{1}\subseteq S
such that
S_{1}
is a column base of
{\cal V}_{SP}\circ S

and
S_{1}\cup P_{1}
is a column base of
{\cal V}_{SP}.

6.

If
P_{1}\subseteq P
is a column base of
{\cal V}_{SP}\times P,

then there exists a column base
P_{2}
of
{\cal V}_{SP}\circ P,

that contains it.

7.

Let
A,B\subseteq S\uplus P,A\cap B=\emptyset,
and let there exist a column base
D_{1}
of
{\cal V}_{SP}
that contains
A
and a column cobase
E_{2}
of
{\cal V}_{SP}
that
contains
B.
Then there exists a column base
D
of
{\cal V}_{SP}
that contains
A
but does not intersect
B.

3.2
Graphs

For a
directed graph
{\cal G}
(graph in brief), the vertex and edge sets are denoted by
V({\cal G}),E({\cal G}),

respectively.
An edge is usually diagrammatically represented with an arrow going from its
positive to its negative endpoint. The
degree
of a node is the number
of edges incident at it, with edges with single endpoints (
self loops
) counted twice. An
isolated
vertex has degree zero. An
undirected path
between vertices
v_{0},v_{k}
of
{\cal G}
is a
sequence
(v_{0},e_{0},v_{1},e_{1},\cdots,e_{k-1},v_{k}),
where

e_{i},i=0,\cdots,k-1,
is incident at
v_{i}.

A graph is said to be
connected
, if there exists an undirected path between every pair of nodes. Otherwise it is said to be
disconnected
.
A subgraph
{\cal G}_{1}
of a graph
{\cal G},
is a graph where
V({\cal G}_{1})\subseteq V({\cal G}),E({\cal G}_{1})\subseteq E({\cal G}),
and edges of
{\cal G}_{1}
have the same positive and negative endpoints as in
{\cal G}.

A disconnected graph has
connected components
which are individually connected with no edges between the components.

A
loop
is a connected subgraph with the degree of each node equal to
2.

An
orientation
of a loop is a sequence of all its edges such that each
edge has a common end point with the edge succeeding it, the first edge
being treated as succeeding the last. Two orientations in which the succeeding edge to a given
edge agree are treated as the same so that there are only two possible
orientations for a loop. The relative orientation of an edge
with respect to that of the loop is positive, if the orientation
of the loop agrees with the direction (positive node to negative node)
of the edge and negative if opposite.

A
tree
subgraph of a graph is a connected sub-graph of the original graph with no loops. The set of edges of a tree subgraph is called a
tree
and its edges are called
branches
. A
spanning tree
is a maximal tree with respect to the edges of a connected graph.
A
cotree
of a graph is the (edge set) complement of a spanning tree of the connected graph.

A
cutset
is a minimal
subset of edges which when deleted from the graph increases the count of connected components by one.
Deletion of the edges of a cutset breaks exactly one of the components
of the graph, say
{\cal G}_{1},
into two, say
{\cal G}_{11},{\cal G}_{12}.
A cutset can be oriented in one
of two ways corresponding to the ordered pair
({\cal G}_{11},{\cal G}_{12})
or the ordered pair
({\cal G}_{12},{\cal G}_{11}).

The relative orientation of an edge
with respect to that of the cutset is positive if the orientation, say
({\cal G}_{11},{\cal G}_{12}),

of the cutset agrees with the direction (positive node to negative node)
of the edge and negative if opposite.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a graph with
S:\equiv E(\mathcal{G})
and let
T\subseteq S.
Then

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}sub(S-T)}
denotes the graph obtained by removing the edges
T
from
\mathcal{G}.

This operation is referred to also as
deletion
or
open circuiting of the edges
T.

The graph
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}\circ(S-T)}
is obtained by removing the isolated vertices from
\mathcal{G}sub(S-T).

The graph
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{G}\times(S-T)}
is obtained by removing the edges
T
from
\mathcal{G}
and fusing the end vertices of the removed edges. If any isolated vertices (i.e., vertices not incident on any edges) result, they are deleted.
Equivalently, one may first build
\mathcal{G}subT,

and treat each of its connected components, including the isolated nodes
as a ‘supernode’ of another graph with edge set
S-T.

If any of the supernodes is isolated, it is removed.
This would result in
{\mathcal{G}\times(S-T)}.

This operation is referred to also as
contraction
or
short circuiting of the edges
T.

We refer to
({\cal G}\times T)\circ W,({\cal G}\circ T)\times W
respectively, more simply by
{\cal G}\times T\circ W,{\cal G}\circ T\times W.

If disjoint edge sets
A,B

are respectively deleted and contracted,
the order in which these operations are performed
can be seen to be irrelevant.
Therefore,
{\cal G}\circ(S-A)\times(S-(A\uplus B))={\cal G}\times(S-B)\circ(S-(A\uplus B)).

(Note that
\times,\circ
are also used to denote vector space operations. However, the context would make clear
whether the objects involved are graphs or vector spaces.)

Kirchhoff’s Voltage Law (KVL)
for a graph states that the sum of the signed voltages of
edges
around an oriented loop is zero - the sign of the voltage of an edge
being positive if the edge orientation agrees with the orientation of the loop
and negative if it opposes.

Kirchhoff’s Current Law (KCL)
for a graph states that the sum of the signed currents leaving
a node is zero, the sign of the current of an edge being positive if
its positive endpoint is the node in question.

We refer to the space of vectors
v_{S},
which satisfy Kirchhoff’s Voltage Law (KVL) of the graph
\mathcal{G},

by
\boldsymbol{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G})}
and to the space of vectors
i_{S},
which satisfy Kirchhoff’s Current Law (KCL) of the graph
\mathcal{G},

by
\boldsymbol{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G})}.

These vector spaces will, unless otherwise stated, be taken as
over
\Re.

The following are useful results on vector spaces associated with graphs.

Theorem 3
.

Tellegen’s Theorem

[
34
]
,
[
27
]
Let
{\cal G}
be a graph on edge set
S.
Then
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G})=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G})^{\perp}).

Lemma 4
.

[
35
]

Let
{\cal G}
be a graph on edge set
S.

Let
W\subseteq T\subseteq S.

1.

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G}\circ T)=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G}))\circ T,\ \ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G}\times T)=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G}))\times T,\ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G}\circ T\times W)=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G}))\circ T\times W;

2.

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G}\circ T)=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G}))\times T,\ \ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G}\times T)=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G}))\circ T,\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G}\times T\circ W)=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G}))\circ T\times W.

3.

A subset of columns is a column base of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G})
iff it is a tree of

{\cal G}.

4.

A subset of columns is a column base of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G})
iff it is a cotree of

{\cal G}.

(Proof also available at
[
19
]
).

3.3
Sum and Intersection

Let
\mathcal{K}_{SP}
,
\mathcal{K}_{PQ}
be collections of vectors on sets
S\uplus P,

P\uplus Q,
respectively, where
S,P,Q,
are pairwise disjoint. The
sum

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SP}+\mathcal{K}_{PQ}}
of
\mathcal{K}_{SP}
,
\mathcal{K}_{PQ}
is defined over
S\uplus P\uplus Q,
as follows:

\mathcal{K}_{SP}+\mathcal{K}_{PQ}:\equiv\{(f_{S},f_{P},0_{Q})+(0_{S},g_{P},g_{Q}),\textrm{ where }(f_{S},f_{P})\in\mathcal{K}_{SP},(g_{P},g_{Q})\in\mathcal{K}_{PQ}\}.

Thus,

\mathcal{K}_{SP}+\mathcal{K}_{PQ}:\equiv(\mathcal{K}_{SP}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{Q})+({\mathbf{0}}_{S}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{PQ}).

The
intersection

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SP}\cap\mathcal{K}_{PQ}}
of
\mathcal{K}_{SP}
,
\mathcal{K}_{PQ}
is defined over
S\uplus P\uplus Q,
where
S,P,Q,
are pairwise disjoint, as follows:

\mathcal{K}_{SP}\cap\mathcal{K}_{PQ}:\equiv\{f_{SPQ}:f_{SPQ}=(f_{S},h_{P},g_{Q}),

\textrm{ where }(f_{S},h_{P})\in\mathcal{K}_{SP},(h_{P},g_{Q})\in\mathcal{K}_{PQ}.\}.

Thus,

\mathcal{K}_{SP}\cap\mathcal{K}_{PQ}:\equiv(\mathcal{K}_{SP}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{Q})\cap(\mbox{$\cal F$}_{S}\oplus\mathcal{K}_{PQ}).

It is immediate from the definition of the operations that sum and intersection of
vector spaces remain vector spaces.

The following set of identities is useful.

Theorem 5
.

Let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\prime}_{S}
be vector spaces. Then

1.

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\prime}_{S})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\prime}_{S})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\prime}_{S});

2.

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{A})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{A}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})+r[(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{A}\times(A\cap B))\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\times(A\cap B))];

3.

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B})^{\perp}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A})^{\perp}\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B})^{\perp};

4.

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B})^{\perp}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A})^{\perp}+(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B})^{\perp}.

5.

(a)
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B})\circ X=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A}\circ X+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\circ X,X\subseteq A\cap B;

(b)
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B})\times X=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{A}\times X\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\times X,X\subseteq A\cap B.

3.4
Networks and multiports

A (static)
electrical network
\mathcal{N},
or a ‘network’ in short, is a pair
(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{K}),
where
\mathcal{G}

is a directed graph with vertex set
V({\cal G})
and edge set
E({\cal G})

and
\mathcal{K},
called the
device characteristic
of the network, is a collection of pairs of vectors
(v_{S^{\prime}},i_{S"}),S:\equiv E({\cal G})
where
S^{\prime},S"
are disjoint copies of
S,\

v_{S^{\prime}},i_{S"}
are real or complex vectors on the edge set of the graph.
In this paper, for the most part, we deal with affine device characteristic
and with real vectors, unless otherwise stated. When the device characteristic
{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is affine,
we say the network is
linear
. If
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is the vector space
translate of
{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"},
we say that
{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is the
source accompanied

form of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}.
An affine space
{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is said to be
proper

iff its vector space
translate
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}
has dimension
|S^{\prime}|=|S"|.

A
solution
of
\mathcal{N}:\equiv({\cal G},{\cal K})
on graph
{\cal G}

is a pair

(v_{S^{\prime}},i_{S"}),S:\equiv E({\cal G})
satisfying

v_{S^{\prime}}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G}),\ \ i_{S"}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G})

(KVL,KCL) and
(v_{S^{\prime}},i_{S"})\in\mathcal{K}.

The KVL,KCL conditions are also called
topological
constraints.
Let
S^{\prime},S"
be disjoint copies of
S,

let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}}:\equiv\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}(\mathcal{G}).
Then by Theorem
3
, we have
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{S^{\prime}})_{S"}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}(\mathcal{G}).
Let
\ {\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}
be the device characteristic of
{\cal N}.

The set of solutions of
\mathcal{N}
may be written, using the extended
definition of intersection as

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}}\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{S^{\prime}})_{S"}\cap{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}=[\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{S^{\prime}})_{S"}]\cap{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}.

This has the form ‘[Solution set of topological constraints]
\cap
[Device characteristic]’.

A
multiport
\mathcal{N}_{P}
on graph
{\cal G}_{SP}
and device characteristic
{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is an ordered pair
({\cal G}_{SP},{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}),
i.e., a network with some subset
P
of its
edges which have no device characteristic constraints, specified as
ports
.
The multiport is said to be
linear
iff its device characteristic is affine.
Let
{\cal N}_{P}
be on graph
{\cal G}_{SP}
with device characteristic
{\cal K}.

Let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}:\equiv(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}},
so that
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}})_{S"P"}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S"P"},
and let
{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"},
be the affine device characteristic
on the edge set
S.
The device characteristic of
\mathcal{N}_{P},

when the latter is regarded as a network,
would be
{\cal K}:\equiv{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{P^{\prime}P"}.

However, we would refer to
{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}
as the device characteristic
of the multiport
{\cal N}_{P}.

The set of solutions of
\mathcal{N}_{P}
may be writen, using the extended
definition of intersection as

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}})_{S"P"}\cap{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}=[\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}})_{S"P"}]\cap{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}.

We say the multiport is
consistent
iff its set of solutions
is nonvoid.

The multiport
\mathcal{N}_{P}
would impose a relationship
between

v_{P^{\prime}},i_{P"}.
We call this the
multiport behaviour
(port behaviour for short)
{{\cal K}}_{P^{\prime}P"}
at
P,
of
{\cal N}_{P},
defined
by

{{\cal K}}_{P^{\prime}P"}:\equiv[(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}})_{S"P"})\cap{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}]\circ P^{\prime}P".

When the device characteristic of
\mathcal{N}_{P}
is affine, its multiport behaviour
{{\cal K}}_{P^{\prime}P"}
at
P
would be
affine if it were not void.

Remark 2
.

The usual definition of multiport behaviour would involve a change of sign
of the current variables. In the interest of readability, we have avoided this, since it is not relevant for this paper.

Let the multiports
{\cal N}_{RP},{{\cal N}}_{\tilde{P}Q}
be on graphs
{\cal G}_{RSP},{\cal G}_{\tilde{P}MQ}
respectively, with the primed and double primed sets obtained from

R,S,P,\tilde{P},M,Q,
being pairwise disjoint,
and let them have device characteristics
{\cal K}^{S},{{\cal K}}^{M}
respectively.
Let
{\cal K}^{P\tilde{P}}
denote a collection of vectors
{\cal K}^{P\tilde{P}}_{P^{\prime}\tilde{P}^{\prime}P"\tilde{P}"}.

The multiport
\boldsymbol{[{\cal N}_{RP}\oplus{{\cal N}}_{\tilde{P}Q}]\cap{\cal K}^{P\tilde{P}}},

with ports
R\uplus Q
obtained by
connecting
{\cal N}_{RP},{{\cal N}}_{\tilde{P}Q}

through
{\cal K}^{P\tilde{P}}
,
is on graph
{\cal G}_{RSP}\oplus{\cal G}_{\tilde{P}MQ}
with device characteristic

{\cal K}^{S}\oplus{{\cal K}}^{M}\oplus{\cal K}^{P\tilde{P}}.

3.5
Matched and Skewed Composition

In this section we introduce an operation between collections of vectors
motivated by the connection of multiport behaviours across ports.

Let
{\cal K}_{SP},{\cal K}_{PQ},
be collections of vectors respectively on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,
with
S,P,Q,
being pairwise disjoint.

The
matched composition

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SP}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{PQ}}
is on
S\uplus Q
and is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{SP}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{PQ}

\displaystyle:\equiv\{(f_{S},g_{Q}):(f_{S},h_{P})\in{\cal K}_{SP},(h_{P},g_{Q})\in{\cal K}_{PQ}\}.

Matched composition is referred to as matched sum in
[
19
]
.

The
skewed composition

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{K}_{SP}\rightleftharpoons\mathcal{K}_{PQ}}
is on
S\uplus Q
and is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{SP}\rightleftharpoons\mathcal{K}_{PQ}

\displaystyle:\equiv\{(f_{S},g_{Q}):(f_{S},h_{P})\in{\cal K}_{SP},(-h_{P},g_{Q})\in{\cal K}_{PQ}\}.

We note that the port behaviour of the multiport
{\cal N}_{P}:\equiv({\cal G}_{SP},{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"})
can be written as

[\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}})_{S"P"}]\leftrightarrow{\cal K}_{S^{\prime}S"}.

The following result is from
[
19
]
. We give a new proof.

Theorem 6
.

Let
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal V}_{PQ},
be vector spaces on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,
respectively,
with
S,P,Q,
being pairwise disjoint.
Then

r({\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{PQ})=r({\cal V}_{SP}\times S)+r({\cal V}_{PQ}\times Q)+r({\cal V}_{SP}\circ P\cap{\cal V}_{PQ}\circ P)-r({\cal V}_{SP}\times P\cap{\cal V}_{PQ}\times P).

Proof.

Here we have used part 2 of Theorem
2

and part 2 of Theorem
5
.

r({\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{PQ}):\equiv r(({\cal V}_{SP}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q})\times SQ)=r({\cal V}_{SP}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q})-r(({\cal V}_{SP}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q})\circ P)

=r({\cal V}_{SP})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q})-r({\cal V}_{SP}\times P\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\times P)-r({\cal V}_{SP}\circ P+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\circ P)

=r({\cal V}_{SP})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q})-r({\cal V}_{SP}\times P\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\times P)-r({\cal V}_{SP}\circ P)-r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\circ P)+r(({\cal V}_{SP}\circ P)\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\circ P))

=[r({\cal V}_{SP})-r({\cal V}_{SP}\circ P)]+[r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q})-r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\circ P)]-r({\cal V}_{SP}\times P\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\times P)+r(({\cal V}_{SP}\circ P)\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\circ P))

=r({\cal V}_{SP}\times S)+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\times Q)+r({\cal V}_{SP}\circ P\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\circ P)-r({\cal V}_{SP}\times P\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\times P).

The result follows noting that

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\times Q=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{PQ}\times Q,\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\circ P=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{PQ}\circ P,\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-P)Q}\times P=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{PQ}\times P.

∎

The following result is immediate from the definition of matched
and skewed composition.

Theorem 7
.

Let
{\cal K}_{SP},{\cal K}_{PQ}
be collections of vectors on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,
respectively.
Then,

{\cal K}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal K}_{PQ}\ \ =\ \ ({\cal K}_{SP}+{\cal K}_{(-P)Q})\times SQ=({\cal K}_{SP}\cap{\cal K}_{PQ})\circ SQ;

{\cal K}_{SP}\rightleftharpoons{\cal K}_{PQ}\ \ =\ \ ({\cal K}_{SP}\cap{\cal K}_{(-P)Q})\circ SQ=({\cal K}_{SP}+{\cal K}_{PQ})\times SQ.

4
Implicit Inversion and Implicit Duality

In this section we present two results which can be regarded as lying at the foundation
of Implicit Linear Algebra. They are essentially available in
[
16
]
. More general versions are available in
[
19
]
,
[
26
]
. Proof of the version of implicit inversion theorem given
below is available in
[
26
]
.

4.1
Implicit Inversion Theorem

Theorem 8
.

Let
{\cal V}_{SP}
be a vector space on
S\uplus P
and let
{\cal K}_{PQ},{\cal K}_{SQ}

be collections of vectors with
S,P,Q,
being pairwise disjoint.
Consider the equation

\displaystyle{\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal K}_{PQ}={\cal K}_{SQ},

(1)

with
{\cal K}_{PQ}
treated as unknown.
We have the following.

1.

given
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal K}_{SQ},
there exists
\hat{{\cal K}}_{PQ},
satisfying Equation
1
, only if
{\cal V}_{SP}\circ S\supseteq{\cal K}_{SQ}\circ S

and
{\cal K}_{SQ}+{\cal V}_{SP}\times S\subseteq{\cal K}_{SQ}.

2.

given
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal K}_{SQ},
if
{\cal V}_{SP}\circ S\supseteq{\cal K}_{SQ}\circ S
and

{\cal K}_{SQ}+{\cal V}_{SP}\times S\subseteq{\cal K}_{SQ},

then
\hat{{\cal K}}_{PQ}:\equiv{\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal K}_{SQ}

satisfies the equation.

3.

given
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal K}_{SQ},
assuming that Equation
1
, with
{\cal K}_{PQ}
treated as unknown, is satisfied by some
\hat{{\cal K}}_{PQ},
it is unique if the additional conditions
{\cal V}_{SP}\circ P\supseteq{\cal K}_{PQ}\circ P
and

{\cal K}_{PQ}+{\cal V}_{SP}\times P\subseteq{\cal K}_{PQ}
are imposed.

The case where
{\cal K}_{PQ},{\cal K}_{SQ}
are vector spaces is particularly important
for us. Here we observe that the condition
{\cal K}_{SQ}+{\cal V}_{SP}\times S\subseteq{\cal K}_{SQ},

reduces to
{\cal V}_{SP}\times S\subseteq{\cal V}_{SQ}\times S.

Theorem 9
.

Consider the equation

{\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{PQ}={\cal V}_{SQ},

where
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal V}_{PQ},{\cal V}_{SQ}
are vector spaces respectively on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,S\uplus Q,
with
S,P,Q,
being pairwise disjoint.We then have the following.

1.

given
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal V}_{SQ},
there exists
{\cal V}_{PQ},
satisfying the equation only if
{\cal V}_{SP}\circ S\supseteq{\cal V}_{SQ}\circ S
and
{\cal V}_{SP}\times S\subseteq{\cal V}_{SQ}\times S.

2.

given
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal V}_{SQ},
if
{\cal V}_{SP}\circ S\supseteq{\cal V}_{SQ}\circ S
and
{\cal V}_{SP}\times S\subseteq{\cal V}_{SQ}\times S,
then
\hat{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{PQ}:\equiv{\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{SQ}

satisfies the equation.

3.

given
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal V}_{SQ},
assuming that the equation
{\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{PQ}={\cal V}_{SQ}

is satisfied by some
\hat{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{PQ},
it is unique if the additional conditions

{\cal V}_{SP}\circ P\supseteq{\cal V}_{PQ}\circ P
and
{\cal V}_{SP}\times P\subseteq{\cal V}_{PQ}\times P

are imposed.

The following is a useful affine space version of Theorem
8

[
26
]
.

Theorem 10
.

Let
{\cal A}_{SP},{\cal A}_{PQ}
be affine spaces on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,
where
S,P,Q

are pairwise disjoint sets. Let
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal V}_{PQ}
respectively, be the vector
space translates of
{\cal A}_{SP},{\cal A}_{PQ}.
Let
{\cal A}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal A}_{PQ}
be
nonvoid
and let

\alpha_{SQ}\in{\cal A}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal A}_{PQ}.
Then,

1.
{\cal A}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal A}_{PQ}=\alpha_{SQ}+({\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{PQ}).

2.
{\cal A}_{PQ}={\cal A}_{SP}\leftrightarrow({\cal A}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal A}_{PQ})
iff

{\cal V}_{SP}\circ P\supseteq{\cal V}_{PQ}\circ P
and
{\cal V}_{SP}\times P\subseteq{\cal V}_{PQ}\times P.

4.2
Implicit Duality Theorem

Implicit Duality Theorem is a part of network theory folklore.
It also has versions in many different areas of mathematics, such as linear
inequalities and linear equations with integral solutions
[
19
]
, linear and partial differential equations
[
20
,
21
,
24
]
, matroids and submodular functions
[
33
]
.
A version in the context of Pontryagin Duality is available in
[
4
]
.
However, its applications are insufficiently emphasized in the literature.

Theorem 11
.

Implicit Duality Theorem

Let
{\cal V}_{SP},{\cal V}_{PQ}
be vector spaces respectively on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,
with
S,P,Q,
being pairwise disjoint.We then have,

(\mathcal{V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{PQ})^{\perp}\ \hskip 2.84544pt=\hskip 2.84544pt\ \mathcal{V}_{SP}^{\perp}\rightleftharpoons\mathcal{V}_{PQ}^{\perp}.
In particular,

(\mathcal{V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{P})^{\perp}\ \hskip 2.84544pt=\hskip 2.84544pt\ \mathcal{V}_{SP}^{\perp}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{P}^{\perp}.

Proof.

From Theorem
5
, we have that
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{Y})^{\perp}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{X}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{Y}^{\perp})

and, using Theorem
1
, that
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{Y})^{\perp}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{X}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{Y}^{\perp}).

We have, using Theorems
2
,
7
,

({\cal V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal V}_{PQ})^{\perp}=[({\cal V}_{SP}\cap{\cal V}_{PQ})\circ SQ]^{\perp}=[({\cal V}_{SP}^{\perp}+{\cal V}_{PQ}^{\perp})\times SQ]=({\cal V}_{SP}^{\perp}\rightleftharpoons{\cal V}_{PQ}^{\perp}).

For any vector space
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X},
we have
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{(-X)}.

Therefore
(\mathcal{V}_{SP}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{P})^{\perp}=(\mathcal{V}_{SP}^{\perp}\rightleftharpoons\mathcal{V}_{P}^{\perp})=\mathcal{V}_{SP}^{\perp}\leftrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{P}^{\perp}.

∎

Theorem
11
is useful to derive results of the kind
‘if the device characteristic
of a multiport has a certain property, so does the port behaviour’.
To illustrate, consider the case of the device characteristic
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}
being
Dirac
, i.e.,
satisfying the condition
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"})_{S"S^{\prime}}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}^{\perp}.

Note that
[(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S"P"}]^{\perp}=[(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S"P"}]=[(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S"P"}]_{S"P"S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}
(using Theorem
3
),
so that
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S"P"}
is Dirac.

We have the following well known result.

Corollary 12
.

Let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"},
be Dirac. Then so is

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}.

Proof.

Let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{P^{\prime}P"}:\equiv(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}).

We have
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{P^{\prime}P"}^{\perp})_{P"P^{\prime}}=((\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"})^{\perp})_{P"P^{\prime}}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}^{\perp}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}^{\perp})_{P"P^{\prime}}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}^{\perp})_{S"P"S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\leftrightarrow(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}^{\perp})_{S"S^{\prime}}={\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\leftrightarrow{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{S^{\prime}S"}={\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{P^{\prime}P"}.

∎

5
Rigid Multiports

5.1
Introduction

We have seen before that a linear multiport is basically a pair
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},{\cal A}_{B}\},
where
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}
is the solution space of the topological constraints and
{\cal A}_{B}
is the device characteristic.

We are particularly interested in multiports which are well behaved
in the following sense:

(a) they are consistent for arbitrary values of sources,
and
(b) for a given port condition (a voltage-current vector in the
multiport behaviour) there is a unique solution for the
multiport. Practically speaking, it is only such multiports that can be handled by freely
available circuit simulators after some preprocessing.
We call such multiports rigid. In this section we study their connections
which result again in rigid multiports.
Testing for rigidity
is essentially linear algebraic,
although of course, because rigid multiports are well behaved, this labour can be outsourced to circuit simulators. However, in some important special cases the computation can be made
entirely matroidal, the matroids being very simple.
Fortunately, rigidity of matroid pairs can be studied in a manner
entirely parallel to the study of rigid affine pairs. This we do in Subsection
6.2

and the two studies are brought together
in Subsection
7
and
B
,
to yield simple sufficient conditions for
multiports involving controlled sources to be rigid and their port behaviour to have a hybrid representation.

Definition 13
.

Let multiport
{\cal N}_{P}:\equiv({\cal G}_{SP},{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}),

where

{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}S"}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}.

The multiport
{\cal N}_{P}
is said to be
rigid

iff every multiport
\hat{{\cal N}}_{P}:\equiv({\cal G}_{SP},\hat{{\cal A}}_{S^{\prime}S"}),

where
\hat{{\cal A}}_{S^{\prime}S"}=\hat{\alpha}_{S^{\prime}S"}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"},

has a non void set of
solutions
and has a unique solution corresponding to every vector in its multiport behaviour.
A
multiport
{\cal N}_{P}:\equiv({\cal G}_{SP},{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"})
is said to be
proper
iff
it is rigid and
{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is proper.

Let network
{\cal N}:\equiv({\cal G}_{S},{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}),

where

{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}=\alpha_{S^{\prime}S"}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}.

We say
{\cal N}
is
rigid
, equivalently
proper
, iff every network
\hat{{\cal N}}:\equiv({\cal G}_{S},\hat{{\cal A}}_{S^{\prime}S"}),

where
\hat{{\cal A}}_{S^{\prime}S"}=\hat{\alpha}_{S^{\prime}S"}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"},

has a unique solution.

Example 14
.

A multiport
{\cal N}_{P}
is rigid in the following cases.
There are no topological conditions on the port edges
P.

They may contain loops and cutsets.

1.

{\cal N}_{P}
has only positive resistors, voltage and current
sources and the voltage sources do not form loops and current sources
do not form cutsets. In this case the dimension of the port behaviour

{\cal A}_{P^{\prime}P"}
is
|P|.

2.

{\cal N}_{P}
has only resistors and norators which form neither loops nor cutsets. In this case the dimension of the port behaviour

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{P^{\prime}P"}
can be as large as
2|P|.

3.

{\cal N}_{P}
has only resistors and nullators.
In this case the dimension of the port behaviour

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{P^{\prime}P"}
can be as small as
0.

4.

{\cal N}_{P}
has only nonzero resistors, independent and controlled sources,
and the values of resistors and the parameters of the controlled
sources are algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}}.
The independent
and controlled sources satisfy topological conditions
of Theorem
68
.
In this case the dimension of the port behaviour

{\cal A}_{P^{\prime}P"}
is
|P|.

{\cal N}_{P}
is not rigid in the following cases.

1.

{\cal N}_{P}
has voltage sources that form loops or current sources
that form cutsets. In this case the multiport will have no solution
for some choice of source values.

2.

{\cal N}_{P}
has norators which contain loops or cutsets.
In this case the port conditions cannot determine the internal conditions uniquely.

It can be shown that any given affine space
{{\cal A}}_{P^{\prime}P"},

can be realized as the multiport behaviour of a rigid
multiport.

5.2
Rigid pairs of affine spaces

Definition 15
.

Let
{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}
be affine spaces on sets
A\uplus B,B\uplus C,
respectively, A,B,C being pairwise disjoint.
Further, let
{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}
have vector space translates
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC},

respectively.

We say the pair
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
has the
full sum property
iff

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}.

We say the pair
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
has the
zero intersection property
iff

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

We say
{\cal A}_{AB}
is
rigid with respect to

{\cal A}_{BC},
or that the pair
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
is
rigid,

iff it has the full sum property and the zero intersection property.

When
B=\emptyset,
we take
\{{\cal A}_{A},{\cal A}_{C}\}
to be rigid
.

When affine spaces are direct sums of other spaces, it is clear
from the definition that rigidity involving the former can be inferred from
that involving the latter.

Lemma 16
.

The pair
\{{\cal A}_{AB}\oplus{\cal A}_{CD},{\cal A}^{1}_{B}\oplus{\cal A}^{2}_{C}\}
is rigid
iff the pairs
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}^{1}_{B}\},

\{{\cal A}_{CD},{\cal A}^{2}_{C}\}
are rigid.

The next result states
the basic facts about rigid
pairs.

Theorem 17
.

Let
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
be a rigid
pair and let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}
be the vector space translates of
{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC},
respectively. Then

1.

The full sum property of
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
is equivalent to

\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}
being nonvoid, whenever

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}
are the vector space translates of
\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB},\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC},
respectively.
Further,
when the full sum property holds for
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\},

\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}
has vector space translate
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}.

2.

The zero intersection property of
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
is equivalent to
the statement that ,

if
(f_{A},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{AB}\leftrightarrow{\cal A}_{BC}
and
(f_{A},f_{B},f_{C}),(f_{A},f^{\prime}_{B},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{AB}\cap{\cal A}_{BC},

then
f_{B}=f^{\prime}_{B}.

3.

The pair
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
is rigid

iff

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})
and
r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}).

4.

The pair
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
has the zero intersection (full sum) property iff
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{BC}\}
has the full sum (zero intersection) property.
Therefore

\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
is rigid
iff

\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{BC}\}
is rigid.

Proof.

1.
Let
\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}=(\alpha_{A},\alpha_{B})+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}=\beta_{BC}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}.

By the definition of matched composition,
\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}
is nonvoid iff
\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\cap\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}
is nonvoid,

i.e., iff
\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\circ B\ \cap\ \hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}\circ B
is nonvoid,

i.e., iff there exist
\lambda_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B,\sigma_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B,

such that
\alpha_{B}+\lambda_{B}=\beta_{B}+\sigma_{B},

i.e., iff there exist
\lambda_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B,\sigma_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B,

such that
\alpha_{B}-\beta_{B}=\sigma_{B}-\lambda_{B}.

Clearly when
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B},

there exist
\lambda_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B,\sigma_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B,

such that
\alpha_{B}-\beta_{B}=\sigma_{B}-\lambda_{B}

so that
\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}
is nonvoid.

On the other hand, if
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B\neq\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B},

there exist
\alpha_{B},\beta_{B}
such that
\alpha_{B}-\beta_{B}\notin\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B,
so that
\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\cap\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}
and therefore

\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}
is void.

By Theorem
10
, if
\hat{{\cal A}}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\hat{{\cal A}}_{BC}
is nonvoid, its vector space translate is
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}.

2.
Let
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B)\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

If
(f_{A},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{AB}\leftrightarrow{\cal A}_{BC}
and

(f_{A},f_{B},f_{C}),(f_{A},f^{\prime}_{B},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{AB}\cap{\cal A}_{BC},

then
(f_{A},f_{B})-(f_{A},f^{\prime}_{B})=(0_{A},(f_{B}-f^{\prime}_{B}))\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},
and similarly
((f_{B}-f^{\prime}_{B}),0_{C})\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC},
so that
(f_{B}-f^{\prime}_{B})\in(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B)\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

Next suppose whenever
(f_{A},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{AB}\leftrightarrow{\cal A}_{BC}
and
(f_{A},f_{B},f_{C}),(f_{A},f^{\prime}_{B},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{AB}\cap{\cal A}_{BC},
we have
f_{B}=f^{\prime}_{B}.

Suppose
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B)\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)\neq{\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

Let
g_{B}\in(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B)\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)
and let
g_{B}\neq 0_{B}.

If
(f_{A},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{AB}\leftrightarrow{\cal A}_{BC},
then there exists
f_{B}
such that
(f_{A},f_{B})\in{\cal A}_{AB}
and

(f_{B},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{BC}.

Clearly
(f_{A},f_{B}+g_{B})\in{\cal A}_{AB}
and

(f_{B}+g_{B},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{BC},

so that we must have
(f_{A},f_{B}+g_{B},f_{C})\in{\cal A}_{AB}\cap{\cal A}_{BC}.

But this means
f_{B}=f_{B}+g_{B},

a contradiction.
We conclude that
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B)\cap(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

3. Let
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
be rigid
.
Then
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
and
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

Now, using part 2 of Theorem
5
and the zero intersection property,

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})-r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}).

Next
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
iff
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B)^{\perp}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}^{\perp},
i.e.,

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

Using the above argument, we therefore have

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}).

On the other hand, suppose
r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})
and
r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}).

The former condition implies
r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)=0,
i.e.,
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

and the latter implies
r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}\times B)=0,
i.e.,

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B},

i.e.,
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}\times B)^{\perp}=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
(using Theorems
5
,
2
).

4. We have, as before,

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B)^{\perp}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}\times B\ \cap\ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}\times B=\mbox{$\cal F$}^{\perp}_{B}={\mathbf{0}}_{B},

and

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B\ \cap\ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B)^{\perp}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}\circ B={\mathbf{0}}^{\perp}_{B}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}.

∎

Remark 3
.

1. Given a pair
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\}
we can always construct a rigid
pair

\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}\}
such that

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}.

We give a construction for
\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}
below.

Since
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B)

=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B),

we can, without loss of generality, take
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B\subseteq\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B.

Split
\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
as
\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},
where
the subspaces
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B,\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},
have zero intersection.
Next split
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}
as
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B},

taking the subspaces
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B,\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B},
to have zero intersection.
Take
\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}:\equiv\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}.

Clearly
\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}.

Next,

\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

Thus
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}\}
is rigid.

We will first show that
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}.

Let
f_{A}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}.

Then there exist
(f_{A},f_{B})\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}
and
f_{B}\in\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}.

We can write
f_{B}
as
f_{B}=f^{1}_{B}+f^{2}_{B},
where
f^{1}_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},f^{2}_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B.
Now
f_{B},f^{2}_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B,
so that

f^{1}_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B
and therefore in
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B}.

This means that
f^{1}_{B}=0_{B}
so that
f_{B}=f^{2}_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}.

It follows that
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}\subseteq\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}.

To see the reverse containment, let
f_{A}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}.

Then there exist
(f_{A},f_{B})\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}
and
f_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}.

We can write
f_{B}
as
f_{B}=f^{3}_{B}+f^{2}_{B},
where
f^{3}_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B,f^{2}_{B}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}.
Clearly,
(f_{A},f_{B})-(0_{A},f^{3}_{B})=(f_{A},f^{2}_{B})\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}

and by definition of
\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B},
we have
f^{2}_{B}\in\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}.
Therefore
f_{A}\in\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}.

We thus see that
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}\}
is rigid
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\tilde{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}=\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}.

Thus rigidity is a property of the pair
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\}

that cannot be inferred from
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{B}

alone.

2. A consequence of part 1 is that whenever a multiport
{\cal N}_{P}
has
a port behaviour
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{P^{\prime}P"},
we can alter the
multiport by changing the device characteristic
so that the resulting multiport
\tilde{{\cal N}_{P}}
also has multiport behaviour
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{P^{\prime}P"},
but is rigid
.
Therefore every multiport behaviour is realizable as that of a rigid
multiport. Use of parts 1 and 2 of Theorem
17
will prove this fact for
affine multiport behaviours also.

3. The above ideas do not appear to be true for matroids in general
unless we can somehow bring in the implicit inversion theorem,
which we know to be false for matroids in general.

The next lemma is of use in characterizing rigid networks without ports
and, in the case of rigid multiports, the dimension of the multiport
of the multiport behaviour.

Lemma 18
.

1.

Let
{\cal A}^{1}_{B},{\cal A}^{2}_{B}
have
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B},

respectively as their vector space translates.

(a)

If
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\}

is rigid,
{\cal A}^{1}_{B}\cap{\cal A}^{2}_{B}
will contain exactly one vector.

(b)

If
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\}
is not rigid, then there exist
\hat{{\cal A}}_{B}^{1},\hat{{\cal A}}_{B}^{2},
with
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B},
respectively, as their vector space translates,
such that
\hat{{\cal A}}_{B}^{1}\cap\hat{{\cal A}}_{B}^{2}
is not a singleton.

(c)

If
r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B})=|B|,
then
the full sum property and zero intersection property are equivalent
and
{{\cal A}}_{B}^{1}\cap{{\cal A}}_{B}^{2}
is a singleton iff
\{{\cal A}^{1}_{B},{\cal A}^{2}_{B}\}

is rigid.

2.

If
\{{\cal A}_{AB},{\cal A}_{BC}\}
is rigid,
then

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})-|B|.

Proof.

1(a). Let
{\cal A}^{1}_{B}:\equiv\alpha_{B}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B}
and let
{\cal A}^{2}_{B}:\equiv\beta_{B}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}.
Suppose
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\}
is rigid.
Let
M,N
be representative matrices of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B},
respectively.
Consider the equation

\alpha^{T}+\lambda^{T}M=\beta^{T}+\sigma^{T}N,

which characterizes the affine space
{\cal A}^{1}_{B}\cap{\cal A}^{2}_{B}.

This equation is equivalent to

\displaystyle\alpha^{T}-\beta^{T}=(-\lambda^{T}|\sigma^{T})\begin{pmatrix}M\\
N\end{pmatrix}.

(2)

This equation has a unique solution since
M,N
have independent rows and
their ranks add up to
|B|.

1(b). On the other hand, suppose
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{1}_{B},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{2}_{B}\}
is not rigid. Then the
ranks of
M,N
either add up to a value below
|B|
or above it.
In the former case,
we can choose
\alpha^{T},\beta^{T}
in such a way that that the equation
has no solution and in the latter, the equation will not have a unique solution. Thus, in either case,
\hat{{\cal A}}_{B}^{1}\cap\hat{{\cal A}}_{B}^{2}
