(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*};

4.

(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*};

5.

(a)
(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})\circ X=\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\circ X\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\circ X,X\subseteq A\cap B;

(b)
(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})\times X=\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\times X\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\times X,X\subseteq A\cap B.

Proof.

1. A base of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{\prime}_{S}
is a union of two maximally distant
bases of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{\prime}_{S}.

By definition, a base of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{\prime}_{S},

is the intersection of two maximally distant
bases of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{S},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{\prime}_{S}.

The result follows from the identity
|X|+|Y|=|X\cup Y|+|X\cap Y|,

where
X,Y,
are sets.

2. We have
\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}:\equiv(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{B-A})\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{A-B}).

From the previous part, we have

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{B-A})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{A-B})

=r((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{B-A})\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{A-B}))+r((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{B-A})\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{A-B})).

Now it can be seen that a minimal intersection of a pair of bases of

\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{B-A},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{A-B}
is the same as a minimal intersection of a pair of bases of

\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\times(A\cap B),\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\times(A\cap B)

so that

r((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{B-A})\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{A-B}))=r[(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\times(A\cap B))\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\times(A\cap B))]

and the result follows.

Parts 3 and 4 follow from the definition of

\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B},(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*},(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*}.

5(a) follows from the definition of the matroid union operation.

5(b) We have
(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})\times X=((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})\times X)^{**}=((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*}\circ X)^{*}=((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*})\circ X)^{*}=((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\circ X\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*}\circ X)^{*}=((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\circ X)^{*}\wedge((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})^{*}\circ X)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\times X\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}\times X.

∎

Theorem 48
.

Let
{\cal M}_{SP},{\cal M}_{PQ},
be matroids on
S\uplus P,P\uplus Q,
respectively,
with
S,P,Q,
being pairwise disjoint.
Then

r({\cal M}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal M}_{PQ})=r({\cal M}_{SP}\times S)+r({\cal M}_{PQ}\times Q)+r({\cal M}_{SP}\circ P\wedge{\cal M}_{PQ}\circ P)-r({\cal M}_{SP}\times P\wedge{\cal M}_{PQ}\times P).

Proof.

Here we have used part 2 of Theorem
47

and part 2 of Theorem
43
.
r({\cal M}_{SP}\leftrightarrow{\cal M}_{PQ}):\equiv r(({\cal M}_{SP}\vee{\cal M}_{PQ})\times SQ)

=r({\cal M}_{SP}\vee{\cal M}_{PQ})-r(({\cal M}_{SP}\vee{\cal M}_{PQ})\circ P)

=r({\cal M}_{SP})+r({\cal M}_{PQ})-r({\cal M}_{SP}\times P\wedge{\cal M}_{PQ}\times P)-r({\cal M}_{SP}\circ P\vee{\cal M}_{PQ}\circ P)

=r({\cal M}_{SP})+r({\cal M}_{PQ})-r({\cal M}_{SP}\times P\wedge{\cal M}_{PQ}\times P)-r({\cal M}_{SP}\circ P)-r({\cal M}_{PQ}\circ P)+r(({\cal M}_{SP}\circ P)\wedge({\cal M}_{PQ}\circ P))

=[r({\cal M}_{SP})-r({\cal M}_{SP}\circ P)]+[r({\cal M}_{PQ})-r({\cal M}_{PQ}\circ P)]-r({\cal M}_{SP}\times P\wedge{\cal M}_{PQ}\times P)+r(({\cal M}_{SP}\circ P)\wedge({\cal M}_{PQ}\circ P))

=r({\cal M}_{SP}\times S)+r({\cal M}_{PQ}\times Q)+r({\cal M}_{SP}\circ P\wedge{\cal M}_{PQ}\circ P)-r({\cal M}_{SP}\times P\wedge{\cal M}_{PQ}\times P).

∎

The following pair of results are from
[
33
]
.

Theorem 49
.

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ})\times(S\cup Q)=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ})\circ(S\cup Q)

Theorem 50
.

(Implicit Duality Theorem (IDT))

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ})^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}^{*}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ}^{*}

Proof.

\displaystyle(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ})^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ})\times(S\cup Q)]^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ})^{*}\circ(S\cup Q)\mbox{ (using Theorem \ref{thm:dotcrossidentitym}).}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}^{*}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ}^{*})\circ(S\cup Q)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}^{*}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ}^{*})\times(S\cup Q)\mbox{ (using Theorem \ref{thm:matchedpropm}).}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mbox{$\cal M$}_{SP}^{*}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{PQ}^{*}.

∎

We have deliberately written the above results in a form that mimics
that of corresponding results for vector spaces.

The substitution rules are

\displaystyle{Vector\ space\ entity}

\displaystyle{Matroid\ entity}

\displaystyle{Vector\ space\ entity}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}^{\perp}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mbox{$\cal M$}_{X}^{*}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mbox{$\cal F$}_{A}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ {\mathbf{0}}_{A}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ {\mathbf{0}}_{A}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\oplus\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{Y}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mbox{$\cal M$}_{X}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{Y}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{Y}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{Y}

The corresponding pairs are
Theorems
42
,
1
, Theorems
43
,

, Theorems

,

, Lemmas
46
,

,

,

,

48
,

,

,

and Theorems

,

.

6.2
Rigidity of matroid pairs

We show in this subsection, that there is a notion analogous to rigidity
of vector space pairs in the matroid context and the properties of
rigid pairs of matroids mimic those of rigid pairs of vector spaces.
This fact is used finally in Theorem
56
, to examine under what
conditions rigidity of a vector space pair can be inferred from
that of the associated matroid pair.

Definition 51
.

Let
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}
be matroids on sets
A\uplus B,B\uplus C,
respectively, A,B,C being pairwise disjoint.
We say the pair
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
has the
full sum property
iff

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}.

We say the pair
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
has the
zero intersection property
iff

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

We say
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}
is
rigid with respect to

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC},
or that the pair
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is
rigid,

iff it has the full sum property and the zero intersection property.

When
B=\emptyset,
we take
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{A},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{C}\}
to be rigid
.

When matroids are direct sums of other matroids, it is clear from the definition that
rigidity involving the former can be inferred from rigidity involving the
latter.

Lemma 52
.

The pair
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{CD},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{C}\}
is rigid
iff the pairs
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B}\},

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{CD},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{C}\}
are rigid.

The next result states
the basic facts about rigid
pairs of matroids.

Theorem 53
.

Let
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
be a rigid
pair of matroids.
We then have the following

1.

The full sum property of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is equivalent to

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B},

equivalently, to
there being bases
b_{AB},b_{BC}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC},

respectively such that
b_{AB}\cup b_{BC}\supseteq B.

2.

The zero intersection property of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is equivalent to
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B},

equivalently, to there being bases
b_{AB},b_{BC}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC},

respectively such that
b_{AB}\cap b_{BC}=\emptyset.

3.

The pair
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is rigid

iff

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})
and
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}).

4.

The pair
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
has the zero intersection (full sum) property iff
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{BC}\}
has the full sum (zero intersection) property.
Therefore

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is rigid
iff

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{BC}\}
is rigid.

5.

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is rigid
iff
there exist disjoint bases
b_{AB},b_{BC}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}
respectively
such that
b_{AB}\cup b_{BC}\supseteq B.

Proof.

1.
Note that
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}:\equiv(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{C})\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{A}).

It is clear that
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\circ B,
so that
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
is equivalent to

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}.

The latter statement is the same as
there being bases
b_{AB},b_{BC}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC},

respectively such that
b_{AB}\cup b_{BC}\supseteq B.

2. Note that
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}:\equiv(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{C})\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{A}).

We have
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B)\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\times B
(using part 5 of Theorem
47
),
so that

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B)\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}
is equivalent to

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

The latter statement is the same as
there being bases
b_{AB},b_{BC}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC},

respectively such that
b_{AB}\cap b_{BC}=\emptyset.

3. Let
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
be rigid
.
Then
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
and
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

Now, using Theorem
47
and the zero intersection property,

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})-r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}).

Next
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
iff
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}^{*},
i.e.,

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

Using the above argument, we therefore have

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}).

On the other hand, suppose
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})
and
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}+\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*})

=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}).

The former condition implies
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)=0,
i.e.,
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

and the latter implies
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\times B)=0,
i.e.,

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B},

i.e.,
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\times B)^{*}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
(using parts 3 and 4 of Theorems
47
,
43
).

4. We have,

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B\ \wedge\ \mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\times B.
Therefore
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}

iff
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B\ \wedge\ \mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B}.

Next

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\ \wedge\ \mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\circ B.
Therefore
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\ \wedge\ \mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B}
iff
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}.

5.
Let
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
be rigid and let

b_{AC}
be a base of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\times AC.

Then there exists a base
b_{ABC}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}
such that

b_{AC}=b_{ABC}\cap(A\uplus C).

Since
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is rigid, by part 3 above, we must have

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}).

Therefore
there exist disjoint bases
b_{AB},b_{BC}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC},
respectively
such that
b_{ABC}=b_{AB}\uplus b_{BC}.

We have,

|b_{AC}|=|b_{AB}\uplus b_{BC}|-|(b_{AB}\uplus b_{BC})\cap B|=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\times AC)

=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})-r((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\circ B)=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})-|B|
(using full sum property of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
).
We conclude that
(b_{AB}\uplus b_{BC})\cap B=B.

Thus rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}

implies that there exist disjoint bases of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}

which cover
B.

On the other hand suppose that there exist disjoint bases of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}

which cover
B.
It is clear that we must have

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})

and
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{BC}),

so that the rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
follows.

∎

The next lemma is of use for characterizing rigid networks without ports
and, in the case of rigid multiports, the dimension of the multiport
of the multiport behaviour. Proof is given in
A
.

Lemma 54
.

1.

Let
\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}
be matroids on
B.

If
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})=|B|,
then
the full sum property and zero intersection property are equivalent

2.

If
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is rigid
then

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})-|B|.

The following result is parallel to Theorem
24
.
Its proof is a line by line translation of that of Theorem
24
and is shifted to
A
.

Theorem 55
.

Let
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T},
be matroids on
W\uplus T\uplus V,V,T,

respectively.
Then
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}\}
is rigid

1.

iff

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}\},

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\}
are rigid;

2.

iff

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}\}
is rigid,
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\},

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\}
satisfy the full sum and zero intersection
properties respectively;

3.

iff
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}\},

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\},

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\}
are rigid.

6.3
Testing rigidity of vector space pairs through associated matroid pairs

Matroids associated with rigid pairs of vector spaces are always rigid.
But under certain
commonly occurring situations, the converse is also true. This fact is useful for testing the
rigidity of the original pairs of vector spaces avoiding linear algebraic computations.

Theorem 56
.

Let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}
be vector spaces on
A\uplus B,B\uplus C,
respectively
with
A,B,C
pairwise disjoint.
We then have the following.

1.

If
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\}
is rigid then so is
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\}.

2.

Suppose
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})
and

\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{BC}).

Then

(a)

\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})=(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}))^{*}\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))^{*}

(b)

\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC});

(c)

\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\}
is rigid iff
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\}

is rigid.

Proof.

We will use the following facts.

(a)
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\circ Z)=(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}))\circ Z,\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\times Z)=(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}))\times Z,

(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X})^{\perp})=(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}))^{*},Z\subseteq X
(Lemma
45
).

(b)
\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{X}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{X}:\equiv((\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{X})^{*}\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{X})^{*})^{*}.

When
A,B,C
are pairwise disjoint,
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}:\equiv(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{C})\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{A})

=((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{C})^{*}\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\oplus\mbox{$\cal F$}_{A})^{*})^{*}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{C})\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\oplus{\mathbf{0}}_{A}))^{*}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*})^{*}
(Theorem
50
).

(c) When
A,B,C
are pairwise disjoint,
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\circ B=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B),(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\times B=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B)\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)
(Theorem
47
).

(d)
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))\leq r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})).

1.
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\}
is rigid iff

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})
and

r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})+r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})

=r(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}),

by part 4 of Theorem
17
.
Therefore, if
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\}
is rigid
then we have,

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}))+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))\leq r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))
and

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}))+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}))

=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}))\leq r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})).

But, by definition of the matroid union operation,
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}))+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))\geq r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))

and
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}))+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}))

\geq r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})).

We therefore conclude that

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}))+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))

and
r((\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}))^{*})+r((\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))^{*})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}))+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}))=r((\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}))^{*}\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))^{*})

and by part 3 of Theorem
53
, it follows that
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\}
is rigid.

2(a) We are given that
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}).

We then have

\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}((\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})^{\perp})=(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp}))^{*}

=((\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}))^{*}\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))^{*})^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}),

using facts (a) and (b).

Similarly, from
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{BC}),
it follows that

\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}^{\perp}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}^{\perp})=(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}))^{*}\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))^{*}.

2(b) We have
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}((\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\times B)=(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))\times B

=(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))\times B=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}),

using the hypothesis of the theorem, fact (a), and the definition of
the ‘
\leftrightarrow
’ operation for matroids.

2(c) The pair
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\}
is rigid iff

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\circ B+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\times B\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B},

i.e., iff

(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
and
(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B},

i.e., iff

\mbox{$\cal M$}((\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\circ B)=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
and
\mbox{$\cal M$}((\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\cap\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\times B)={\mathbf{0}}_{B},

ı.e., iff
(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
and
(\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}))\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B}

i.e., iff
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})\}

is rigid, using fact (c), hypothesis of part 2 of the theorem and part 2(a) above.
∎

6.4
Rigidity and matroid duality

We show in this subsection that
when we have matroid duality, we automatically have rigidity.
This suggests
rigidity can be regarded as a weak form of
matroid duality.

Lemma 57
.

Let
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}
be matroids,
A,B,C,
being pairwise disjoint,
such that

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B.

We then have the following.

1.

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is rigid;

2.

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{AB})_{\tilde{A}B}\},
is rigid, where
\tilde{A}
is
a copy of
A
disjoint from
A,B;

Proof.

1.
Since
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B,
we must have

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}.

Now every independent set of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B
is contained
in an independent set of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B.

Therefore
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B},

i.e., the full sum property holds for
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}.

Next
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)^{**}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)^{*}.

Therefore,
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\circ B,

i.e.,
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\circ B)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B.

By the argument above, this means
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B},
i.e., the full sum property holds for
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}^{*}\}.
By part 4 of Theorem
53
, this means that the zero intersection
property holds for
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}.

We conclude that
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\}
is rigid.

2. We note that
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)^{*}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}^{*}\times B=(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{AB})_{\tilde{A}B}\times B.

The result now follows from part 1 above.
∎

Remark 7
.

We have that
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X}^{*}\}
is rigid. By Theorem
50
,
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{A}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B},\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{A}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{B}\},B\subseteq A,
is also rigid.
We have that
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{A},(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{A})^{*}\}
is rigid since
(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{A})^{*}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{A})^{*}\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{A})^{*}.

There appear to be no counterparts to these facts in terms of rigidity
without using duality.

6.5
Computing a column base of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}

Finding a column base of
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}

requires linear algebraic
computations. However, when the conditions of part 2 of Theorem
56
are satisfied,
we have
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}).

Bases of
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC})
and column bases of

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{BC}
are identical.
The problem of computing a base of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}
given
an ‘independence oracle’ of the matroids
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}

is one whose solution is nontrivial but known.
First, we find a pair of maximally distant bases
b^{1}_{B},b^{2}_{B}
for the matroids
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B
using the well known matroid union algorithm essentially due to
J. Edmonds
[
3
]
.
It follows that
b^{1}_{B}\cup b^{2}_{B}
is a basis for
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\circ B=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B.

We then extend these bases to
b^{1}_{AB},b^{2}_{BC}
of
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}

respectively. Then
b^{1}_{AB}\cup b^{2}_{BC}
is a basis of

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}
that contains the basis
b^{1}_{B}\cup b^{2}_{B}

of
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\circ B.
Therefore,
(b^{1}_{AB}-b^{1}_{B})\uplus(b^{2}_{BC}-b^{2}_{B})
is a base of
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})\times(A\uplus C)=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}.

A detailed discussion of the matroid union algorithm may be found in

[
31
]
,
[
19
]
.
Fortunately for us, in the most important cases where we have to find maximally distant bases, we can do so by computing one of them to satisfy some
simple properties.

6.6
Rigid families of matroids

The notion of rigidity of families carries over from vector spaces
to matroids routinely.
For this purpose we first give a definition of rigidity
for families relevant to our problem (‘associative families’) and then justify the definition by describing
the properties of rigid associative families through subsequent lemmas.

Definition 58
.

An
associative family

\mathcal{H}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}},i=1,\cdots,m\}
is a family of matroids where no element in
Y:\equiv\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}Y_{i}
belongs to more than two of the
Y_{i}.

When
Y_{i}=\emptyset,
we will take
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{\emptyset}:\equiv\emptyset.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{nonvoid}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}},i=1,\cdots,t\}
be the subfamily
of
\mathcal{H}
whose elements are non void matroids that belong to

\mathcal{H}.

For
i=1,\cdots,t,
let
Z_{i}
be the subset of elements of
Y_{i}

which belong to none of the
Y_{j},j\neq i
and let
Z:\equiv\bigcup_{i=1}^{t}Z_{i}.

We define
\boldsymbol{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H})}:\equiv(\bigvee_{i=1}^{t}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}))\times Z=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{t}})\times Z.

Definition 59
.

For an associative family
\mathcal{H}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}}\},

the graph
\boldsymbol{{\cal G}_{\mathcal{H}}}
is defined to have vertices
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}},

edges
Y_{1}\cup\cdots\cup Y_{m}
with an edge
e
lying between
vertices
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{j}}
iff
e\in Y_{i}\cap Y_{j}.

We will refer to
{\cal G}_{\mathcal{H}}
as the graph of
\mathcal{H}.

Remark 8
.

We note the following.

1.

The operation
\leftrightarrow(\cdot)
is well defined over associative families and agrees with the corresponding
operation over pairs of matroids.
If
\mathcal{H}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{2}},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{3}}\},
is an associative family, it can be shown that

\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H})=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{2}})\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{3}}=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}}\leftrightarrow(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{2}}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{3}})

[
33
]
.
By induction it follows that, treated as a binary operation,
\leftrightarrow(\cdot)
is associative over an associative family

\mathcal{H}
and, independent of the way the brackets are constructed this operation
reduces to
\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}).

2.

Suppose
\mathcal{H}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}},i=1,\cdots,m\}
is an associative
family with every element of
\bigcup_{i=1}^{m}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}
belonging to exactly
two of the
Y_{i}.
Then
(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}))=\emptyset.
For consistency of notation,
in this case we denote
\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H})
by
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{\emptyset}.

We note that, by the definition of the operations
\vee,\wedge,\leftrightarrow(\cdot),
we must have
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{\emptyset}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X})=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{\emptyset}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X})=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{\emptyset}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X})=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X}.

The following result is known
[
33
]
.

Lemma 60
.

Let
\mathcal{H}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}}\}
be an associative family of matroids. Let
Z
be the subset of elements of
\bigcup_{i}Y_{i}
that belong
to only one of the
Y_{i}.

Then

\boldsymbol{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H})}

:\equiv(\bigwedge_{i=1}^{m}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}))\circ Z=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}})\circ Z.

3.

If the graph
{\cal G}_{\mathcal{H}}
has edges and is connected, then
it does not have isolated vertices. This means
\mathcal{H}
has no element
which is equal to
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{\emptyset}.
Further, if it is connected and has at least one selfloop, no subfamily
\mathcal{H}_{1}
of
\mathcal{H}
would be such that
\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1})=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{\emptyset}.

4.

Let
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{H}_{2}.
Then
\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H})\ =\ (\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}))\leftrightarrow(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2})).

5.

By induction on the size of
\mathcal{H},
one can prove the following generalization of
Theorem
11
:

Theorem 61
.

Let
\mathcal{H}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}},i=1,\cdots,m\}
be an associative family of matroids
and let

\mathcal{H}^{*}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{i}},i=1,\cdots,m\}.

Then

(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}))^{*}=(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{*})).

Definition 62
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{n}}\}
be an associative family of matroids.
We say
{\mathcal{H}}
is
rigid
iff
the following rank conditions are satisfied.

1.

(primal)
r(\Sigma_{i}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})=\Sigma_{i}r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}});

2.

(dual)
r(\Sigma_{i}\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{i}})=\Sigma_{i}r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{i}}).

We note that the definition of rigid families of matroids agrees with that of a pair
of matroids (by part 3 of Theorem
53
).

We have the following simple lemma.

Lemma 63
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{n}}\}
be an associative family of matroids with
{\cal G}_{\mathcal{H}}
connected and with non empty edge set.
If
\mathcal{H}
is rigid then
every subfamily of
{\mathcal{H}}
is rigid.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, it is adequate
to show that
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}}\},m<n
is rigid.
Now
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}})\leq r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}})+\cdots+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}})

and
r(\bigvee_{i=1}^{n}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})\leq r(\bigvee_{i=1}^{m}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})+r(\bigvee_{i=m+1}^{n}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}).

Therefore the primal rank condition of rigidity is true for
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{n}}\}
only if it is true for
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}}\}.

The proof for the dual rank condition is essentially the same.
∎

The following lemma addresses the rigidity of a common class of associative families.

Lemma 64
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X_{n}}\},
and let
\mathcal{H}_{2}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}}\},
with the
X_{i},
being pairwise disjoint and the
Y_{j}

also.
Then the associative family
\mathcal{H}_{1}\uplus\mathcal{H}_{2}
is rigid iff the
pair
\{\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X_{i}},\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{j}}\},
is rigid.

Proof.

We have

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X_{n}})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X_{1}})+\cdots+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X_{n}}),\ r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}})+\cdots+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{n}}),

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{X_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{X_{n}})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{X_{1}})+\cdots+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{X_{n}}),\ r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{m}})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{1}})+\cdots+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{n}}).

The result now follows from part 3 of Theorem
53
.
∎

We now have a result which expresses rigidity of associative families
recursively.

Theorem 65
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{n}}\}
be an associative family of matroids with
{\cal G}_{\mathcal{H}}
connected and with non empty edge set.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be partitioned as
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\uplus{\mathcal{H}_{2}}.

Then

{\mathcal{H}}
is rigid iff
{\mathcal{H}_{1}},{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
and
\{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2})\}

are rigid.

The proof of Theorem
65
is based on the following lemma.

Lemma 66
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}},i=1,\cdots,m\}

and let
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}},i=1,\cdots,t\},t<m,

{\mathcal{H}}_{2}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}},i=t+1,\cdots,m\}

be subfamilies of
{\mathcal{H}}.

Let
Y^{1}=\bigcup^{t}_{i=1}Y_{i}
and let
Y^{2}=\bigcup^{m}_{i=t+1}Y_{i}.

Then
{\mathcal{H}}
satisfies the rank conditions of Definition
34

iff

{\mathcal{H}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
satisfy them and
further

r([\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})\bigwedge[\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2}))=0
and

r([\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})\bigwedge[\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2}))=0.

Proof.

When
Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}=\emptyset,
it is clear that if the rank condition is satisfied
for
{\mathcal{H}_{1}},{\mathcal{H}_{2}}
the lemma is trivially true.
Let
Y_{1}\cap Y_{2}
be
nonvoid.
By Theorem
47
,

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{A}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{A})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B})-r([\mbox{$\cal M$}_{A}\times(A\cap B)]\wedge[\mbox{$\cal M$}_{B}\times(A\cap B)].
Therefore,

r(\bigvee^{m}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})=r(\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})+r(\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})-r([\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})\wedge[\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})).

It follows that
r(\bigvee^{m}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})=\Sigma^{m}_{i=1}r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})

iff
r(\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})=\Sigma^{t}_{i=1}r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}),t<m,

r(\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})=\Sigma^{m}_{i=t+1}r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})

and
r([\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})\wedge[\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2}))=0.

Working with
\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Y_{i}}
and repeating the argument, we get the
second half of the statement of the lemma.
∎

Proof.

(Proof of Theorem
65
)

By Lemma
66
, the primal rank condition of Definition
34
(
r(\bigvee_{i}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})=\Sigma_{i}r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}})
)
holds for

\mathcal{H}
iff it holds for
{\mathcal{H}_{1}},{\mathcal{H}_{2}},
and
r([\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})\wedge[\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2}))=0.

We have
r([\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})\wedge[\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2}))

=r([\bigvee^{t}_{i=1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times Y^{1}\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})\wedge[\bigvee^{m}_{i=t+1}\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{i}}]\times Y^{2}\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})

=(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}))\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2})\wedge(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}))\times(Y^{1}\cap Y^{2}).

Thus the primal rank condition
holds for

\mathcal{H}
iff it holds for
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
the zero intersection property holds for
\{(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1})),(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}))\}.

The dual rank condition for
\mathcal{H}
is the same as the primal rank condition
for
\mathcal{H}^{*}.
When
\mathcal{H}
is associative, so is
\mathcal{H}^{*}.

Working with
\mathcal{H}^{*}

we can show that the primal rank condition holds for

\mathcal{H}^{*}
iff it holds for
\mathcal{H}^{*}_{1},\mathcal{H}^{*}_{2}
and
the zero intersection property holds for

\{(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{*}_{1})),(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{*}_{2}))\}.

By Theorem
61
,

\{(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{*}_{1})),(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{*}_{2}))\}=\{(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}))^{*})),(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}))^{*}))\}.

Now by
by part 4 of Theorem
17
, the
zero intersection property holds for
\{(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}))^{*})),(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}))^{*}))\}

iff
the
full sum property holds
for

\{(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1})),(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}))\}.

We thus see that the primal and dual rank conditions of rigidity hold for
\mathcal{H}
iff they hold for
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2}
and
\{(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1})),(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}))\}
is rigid.
Thus
\mathcal{H}
is rigid iff
\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\{(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1})),(\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}))\}

are rigid.
∎

The following is a more convenient version of Theorem
65
.

Theorem 67
.

Let
{\mathcal{H}}:\equiv\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Y_{m}}\}
be an associative family of matroids with
{\cal G}_{\mathcal{H}}
connected and with non empty edge set.
Let
{\mathcal{H}}
be partitioned as
{\mathcal{H}_{1}}\uplus\cdots\uplus{\mathcal{H}_{n}}.

Then

{\mathcal{H}}
is rigid iff
{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\cdots,{\mathcal{H}_{n}}
and
\{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\cdots,\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{n})\}

are rigid.

Proof.

1. The proof is by induction over
n.
We have seen in Theorem
65
, that the result is true when
n=2.

We then have
{\mathcal{H}}
is rigid iff
{\mathcal{H}_{1}},({\mathcal{H}_{2}}\uplus\cdots\uplus{\mathcal{H}_{n}}),\{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\leftrightarrow({\mathcal{H}_{2}}\uplus\cdots\uplus\mathcal{H}_{n})\}
are rigid.

By induction,
{\mathcal{H}_{2}}\uplus\cdots\uplus{\mathcal{H}_{n}}
is rigid iff
{\mathcal{H}_{2}},\cdots,{\mathcal{H}_{n}}
and
\{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}),\cdots,\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{n})\}

are rigid.

We therefore need only show that, rigidity of
\{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\leftrightarrow({\mathcal{H}_{2}}\uplus\cdots\uplus\mathcal{H}_{n})\}
and

\{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{2}),\cdots,\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{n})\}
implies rigidity of

\{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\cdots,\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{n})\}.

Let us denote
\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\cdots,\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{n}),
respectively by
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is an associative
family, by the definition of
\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{i}),
it is clear that no element belongs to more than two of the
Z_{i}.

We thus need to show that rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}},(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})\}
and
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}\}

implies the rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}\}.

Suppose
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}},(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})\}
and
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}\}

are rigid. Let
P
be the set of all elements which belong
precisely to one of
Z_{2},\cdots,Z_{m}
and let
T:\equiv Z_{1}\cap\bigcup_{i=2}^{m}Z_{i}.
Since no element belongs to more than
two of
Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{m},
it is clear that
T\subseteq P.

From the zero intersection property of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}},(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})\},
we have that

r((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}}\times T)\wedge(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})\times T)=r((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}}\times T)\wedge[(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\vee\cdots\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})\times P\times T])

=r((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}}\times T)\wedge[(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\vee\cdots\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})\times T])=0.

By Theorem
5
, it follows that

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\vee\cdots\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}).

Since
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}\}

is rigid, we have
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\vee\cdots\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}})+\cdots+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}).

Therefore
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}}\vee\cdots\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}})+\cdots+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}).

The proof of the dual rank condition for
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}\}

is by replacing
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{i}}
by
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{i}}^{*}
everywhere in the above proof and by using the fact that rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}\}
is equivalent to rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Z_{2}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}^{*}_{Z_{m}}\}.

We thus see that rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}},(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}}\leftrightarrow\cdots\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}})\}
and
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{2}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}\}

implies the rigidity of
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{1}},\cdots,\mbox{$\cal M$}_{Z_{m}}\}.

This proves that

{\mathcal{H}}
is rigid iff
{\mathcal{H}_{1}},\cdots,{\mathcal{H}_{n}}
and
\{\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{1}),\cdots,\leftrightarrow(\mathcal{H}_{n})\}

are rigid.

∎

7
Connecting rigid multiports to obtain a rigid multiport

In this section we combine the results of Subsections
5.2
and
6.2
to give simple rules for connecting rigid multiports to obtain a rigid multiport, when the device characteristic has a
special form. This form is sufficiently general to capture
commonly obtained device characteristics.

Suppose we have rigid multiports
{\cal N}_{WQ},{\cal N}_{TW}
and we wish to connect
them at ports
W.
How do we test for rigidity of the resulting multiport

{\cal N}_{TQ}?
How do we connect them so that the resulting multiport is rigid?

The general answer is given in Theorems
21
,

24
and
25
.

As in Theorem
25
,
we could have rigid multiports
{\cal N}_{TW},{{\cal N}}_{\tilde{W}Q}
on graphs

{\cal G}_{RSW},{\cal G}_{\tilde{W}MQ}
respectively and device characteristics

{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"},{{\cal A}}_{M^{\prime}M"}
respectively.
The multiport
{\cal N}_{TQ}:\equiv{[{\cal N}_{TW}\oplus{{\cal N}}_{\tilde{W}Q}]\cap{\cal A}^{W\tilde{W}}},
obtained by connecting

{\cal N}_{TW},{{\cal N}}_{\tilde{W}Q}

through
{\cal A}^{W\tilde{W}}
is rigid, provided the pair

\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{R^{\prime}W^{\prime}R"W"}\oplus\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{\tilde{W}^{\prime}Q^{\prime}\tilde{W}"Q"},{\cal A}^{W\tilde{W}}_{W^{\prime}W"\tilde{W}^{\prime}\tilde{W}"}\},

is rigid, where
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{R^{\prime}W^{\prime}R"W"}\oplus\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{\tilde{W}^{\prime}Q^{\prime}\tilde{W}"Q"}
are the vector space translates of port behaviours of
{\cal N}_{TW},{{\cal N}}_{\tilde{W}Q}

respectively.
This test requires solution of linear equations whose nature is very general.
Fortunately, in some important special cases, the test only involves working with
simple matroids and is therefore very efficient.

7.1
Sufficiency of matroidal tests for rigidity of multiports

We simplify the problem of testing for rigidity of multiports by making an assumption that is justified
in practice.
We assume that the parameters associated with the various devices,
such as
resistance
in the case of resistors,
gain
,
transresistance
,
transconductance
in the case
of controlled sources, are not known exactly but may be treated
as being picked at random from an interval of positive width
\epsilon,

about some real value. This width may be as small as we please,
as long as it is nonzero. This is a way of saying that parameters
are not known exactly. Under this condition, we may treat
the parameters to be algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}}.

We remind the reader that elements
x_{i}\in\Re,i=1,\cdots,k
are

algebraically independent
over
\mathbb{Q}
iff
every nontrivial multivariable polynomial in
x_{i}
with coefficients
from
\mathbb{Q}
takes nonzero value.
Essentially, once this assumption is made, we have to
test for rigidity of a pair
\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}\},

which has the property that
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})

and
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{AB}+\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{B})=\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{AB})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{\perp}_{B}).

So, by Theorem
56
, the problem reduces to testing the rigidity of the pair

\{\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AB}),\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B})\}
which turns out to be simple
because both the matroids
are simple, the matroid
\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{B}),
being particularly so.

In order to deal with connection of multiports in a simple way, we use ideas from topological multiport decomposition
[
16
,
19
]
, which we describe below.

Let
{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}
be a graph. Then it is always possible to construct
graphs
{\cal G}_{S_{1}W},{\cal G}_{S_{2}\tilde{W}},{\cal G}_{W\tilde{W}},
where
W,\tilde{W}
are additional sets of edges which
are disjoint from each other as well as disjoint from
S_{1},S_{2},
such that

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}})=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{S_{1}W})\oplus\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{S_{2}\tilde{W}}))\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{W\tilde{W}})
and

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}})=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{S_{1}W})\oplus\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{S_{2}\tilde{W}}))\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{W\tilde{W}}).

We can always choose
W,\tilde{W}
to have size

|W|=|\tilde{W}|=\xi(S_{1},S_{2}):\equiv r({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}\circ S_{1})-r({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}\times S_{1})

=r({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}\circ S_{2})-r({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}\times S_{2}).

If
{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}\circ S_{1},{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}\circ S_{2}

are connected, these graphs would have common vertices whose number would
be equal to
\xi(S_{1},S_{2})+1.
In this case
{\cal G}_{S_{1}W},{\cal G}_{S_{2}W}
can be built as given below.

Let
v_{1},\cdots,v_{k},
be the common vertices between the above mentioned
subgraphs of
{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}.

Let
e_{1},\cdots,e_{k-1},
be edges distinct from those in

S_{1}\uplus S_{2}
with
e_{i}
having end points
v_{i},v_{k}
for
i=1,\cdots,k-1.

We will call
v_{k}
as the datum node and
we will say
{\cal G}_{S_{1}W},{\cal G}_{S_{2}W}
have
node to datum type
of ports
.
In this case,
{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}
is obtained from
{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}\circ S_{1}

and
{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}\circ S_{2}
by identifying the nodes
v_{1},\cdots,v_{k},
in the two graphs and, further, it turns out that

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}})=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{S_{1}W})\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{S_{2}{W}}))
and

\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}})=(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{S_{1}W})\rightleftharpoons\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{S_{2}{W}})).

It may be noted that, in practice, this is the way larger networks
are built from smaller modules.

Let us suppose we have built rigid multiports
{\cal N}_{TW},{\cal N}_{WQ}
on graphs

{\cal G}_{S_{1}W},{\cal G}_{S_{2}W}
respectively, where
S_{1}\supseteq T,S_{2}\supseteq Q.

Further
let
{\cal G}_{S_{1}W}\circ S_{1},{\cal G}_{S_{2}W}\circ S_{2}
be connected and let

{\cal G}_{S_{1}W},{\cal G}_{S_{2}W}
have node to datum type of ports.
Let the device characteristics for both multiports be composed in terms
of resistors, voltage sources, current sources and controlled sources
(CCVS,VCCS,CCCS,VCVS). Note that the first two letters in the acronym
refer to ‘current controlled’ or ‘voltage controlled’ and the last two, to
‘current source’ or ‘voltage source’.
The combined device characteristic is given below.

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I_{\tilde{Y}_{1}^{\prime}}&0_{\tilde{Z}_{1}^{\prime}}&0_{\tilde{Y}_{1}"}&0_{\tilde{Z}_{1}"}\\
0_{\tilde{Y}_{1}^{\prime}}&I_{\tilde{Z}_{1}^{\prime}}&-r_{\tilde{Z}_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{Y}_{1}"}&0_{\tilde{Z}_{1}"}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{\tilde{Y}_{1}^{\prime}}\\
v_{\tilde{Z}_{1}^{\prime}}\\
i_{\tilde{Y}_{1}"}\\
i_{\tilde{Z}_{1}"}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0_{\tilde{Y}_{2}^{\prime}}&0_{\tilde{Z}_{2}^{\prime}}&I_{\tilde{Y}_{2}"}&0_{\tilde{Z}_{2}"}\\
-g_{\tilde{Z}_{2}"\tilde{Y}_{2}^{\prime}}&0_{\tilde{Z}_{2}^{\prime}}&0_{\tilde{Y}_{2}"}&I_{\tilde{Z}_{2}"}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{\tilde{Y}_{2}^{\prime}}\\
v_{\tilde{Z}_{2}^{\prime}}\\
i_{\tilde{Y}_{2}"}\\
i_{\tilde{Z}_{2}"}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}

(4)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I_{\hat{Y}_{1}^{\prime}}&0_{\hat{Z}_{2}^{\prime}}&0_{\hat{Y}_{1}"}&0_{\hat{Z}_{2}"}\\
0_{\hat{Y}_{1}^{\prime}}&0_{\hat{Z}_{2}^{\prime}}&-\alpha_{\hat{Z}_{2}"\hat{Y}_{1}"}&I_{\hat{Z}_{2}"}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{\hat{Y}_{1}^{\prime}}\\
v_{\hat{Z}_{2}^{\prime}}\\
i_{\hat{Y}_{1}"}\\
i_{\hat{Z}_{2}"}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0_{\hat{Y}_{2}^{\prime}}&0_{\hat{Z}_{1}^{\prime}}&I_{\hat{Y}_{2}"}&0_{\hat{Z}_{1}"}\\
-\beta_{\hat{Z}_{1}^{\prime}\hat{Y}_{2}^{\prime}}&I_{\hat{Z}_{1}^{\prime}}&0_{\hat{Y}_{2}"}&0_{\hat{Z}_{1}"}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{\hat{Y}_{2}^{\prime}}\\
v_{\hat{Z}_{1}^{\prime}}\\
i_{\hat{Y}_{2}"}\\
i_{\hat{Z}_{1}"}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}

(5)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}I&-\hat{R}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{R^{\prime}}\\
i_{R"}\end{pmatrix}=0;\begin{pmatrix}I_{E^{\prime}}&0_{E"}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{E^{\prime}}\\
i_{E"}\end{pmatrix}=s_{E};\begin{pmatrix}0_{J^{\prime}}&I_{J"}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{J^{\prime}}\\
i_{J"}\end{pmatrix}=s_{J}.

(6)

The matrices
r_{\tilde{Z}_{1}^{\prime}\tilde{Y}_{1}"},g_{\tilde{Z}_{2}"\tilde{Y}_{2}^{\prime}},\alpha_{\hat{Z}_{2}"\hat{Y}_{1}"},\beta_{\hat{Z}_{1}^{\prime}\hat{Y}_{2}^{\prime}},\hat{R}
are diagonal matrices and their
diagonal entries are together assumed to be algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}}.

It is clear that a device characteristic composed entirely of the above mentioned devices is a proper affine space. A multiport composed only of these devices
is proper, by definition, iff it is rigid.

If we connect
{\cal N}_{TW},{\cal N}_{WQ}
at ports
W

will we obtain a rigid multiport
{\cal N}_{TQ}?

To answer this question, we need to examine whether the pair

\{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AX},\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}\}
is rigid, where
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X}
is the vector space translate of the device characteristic composed of the devices described in
Equations
4
,
5
,
6
and
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AX}:\equiv(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}))_{S_{1}^{\prime}S_{2}^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}))_{S_{1}"S_{2}"}.

Because of the algebraic independence of the above mentioned parameters, and
the structure of the device characteristic, under certain additional
topological conditions, this problem reduces to checking whether the pair
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AX},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X}\},
is rigid, where
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AX}:\equiv\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{AX}),

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X}:\equiv\mbox{$\cal M$}(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{X})

(Theorem
72
in the Appendix).
This checking is done by applying the matroid union algorithm
[
3
,
31
,
19
]
.

In this section we give a simpler result, which gives sufficient conditions
for the multiport
{\cal N}_{P}:\equiv{\cal N}_{TQ}

to be rigid. This result is easy to state, and additionally
the sufficient conditions given are easy to test and practically
speaking, invariably satisfied.
Essentially, we only need to check if the voltage source like branches (controlled voltage source branches
Z_{1}

together with the (voltage zero) controlling current branches
Y_{1}
and the voltage source
branches
E
) do not form a loop and the current source like branches (controlled current source branches
Z_{2}

together with the (current zero) controlling voltage branches
Y_{2}
and the current source
branches
J
) do not form a cutset in the graph
{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}.

Additionally, when these conditions are satisfied, the result states that the multiport behaviour has a
hybrid representation
, i.e., a representation of the form

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}i_{P_{1}"}\\
v_{(P-P_{1})^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&r_{22}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{P_{1}^{\prime}}\\
i_{(P-P_{1})"}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}s_{P_{1}"}\\
s_{(P-P_{1})^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix},

and the corresponding column base is easy to compute directly from the graph
{\cal G}_{S_{1}S_{2}}.
This makes the computation of the multiport behaviour easier
than when the column base is not available.

The proof is given in
B
.

Theorem 68
.

Let
{\cal N}_{P}
be a multiport on graph
{\cal G}_{SP}
whose device characteristic

{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is composed
entirely of voltage sources, current sources, CCVS,VCCS,CCCS,VCVS and resistors as in Equations
4
,
5
,
6
.
Let
Y_{1}:\equiv\tilde{Y}_{1}\uplus\hat{Y}_{1},Y_{2}:\equiv\tilde{Y}_{2}\uplus\hat{Y}_{2},Z_{1}:\equiv\tilde{Z}_{1}\uplus\hat{Z}_{1},Z_{2}:\equiv\tilde{Z}_{2}\uplus\hat{Z}_{2}.

Let the values of resistors
and the controlled source parameters be algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}}.

We then have the following.

1.

{\cal N}_{P}
is a proper multiport only if

(a)

the controlling current branches
Y_{1}
together with the voltage source
branches
E

do not contain a loop of
{\cal G}_{SP}

and

(b)

the controlling voltage branches
Y_{2}
together with the current source
branches
J

do not contain a cutset of
{\cal G}_{SP}.

2.

{\cal N}_{P}
is a proper multiport if

(a)

the set of controlled voltage source branches
Z_{1}

together with the controlling current branches
Y_{1}
and the voltage source
branches
E

do not contain a loop
of
{\cal G}_{SP}

(b)

the set of controlled current source branches
Z_{2}

together with the controlling voltage branches
Y_{2}
and the current source
branches
J

do not contain a cutset of
{\cal G}_{SP}.

3.

Let the above conditions of 2 be satisfied.
Then there exists a
tree
t
of
{\cal G}_{SP}
that contains
Z_{1}\uplus Y_{1}\uplus E

and does not intersect
Z_{2}\uplus Y_{2}\uplus J.

For such a tree, the port behaviour of multiport
{\cal N}_{P}

has a hybrid representation
with
(t\cap P)^{\prime}\uplus(P-t)"
as a column base for the vector space
translate of the behaviour.

Remark 9
.

The sufficient conditions of Theorem
68
are not necessary.
Let
{\cal G}
be a graph on two edges
e_{1},e_{2},
where the edges are in parallel.
Let these be the device ports of a current controlled voltage source.
Then
Y_{1}:\equiv\{e_{1}\},Z_{1}:\equiv\{e_{2}\}
and
Y_{1}\uplus Z_{1}
contains
a loop. Let
r_{e_{1}"e_{2}^{\prime}}\neq 0.
This network has the unique solution

0_{e_{1}^{\prime}e_{2}^{\prime}e_{1}"e_{2}"}.

From a practical point of view the sufficient
conditions are reasonable since, while designing a circuit, one will never force a dependency on controlled voltages
by making them form a loop with predetermined voltages
or on controlled current sources by making them form a cutset with predetermined currents.

7.2
Algorithm for constructing hybrid representation of
the port behaviour

We present below an algorithm for construction of the hybrid representation of the port behaviour of a multiport satisfying the sufficiency conditions in Theorem
68
.

We remind the reader that hybrid representation for a port behaviour
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{P^{\prime}P"}
is a representative matrix of the form

\displaystyle(F_{P_{1}^{\prime}}|F_{P_{2}^{\prime}}|F_{P_{1}"}|F_{P_{2}"})=\begin{pmatrix}I&h_{21}^{T}&g_{11}^{T}&0\\
0&r_{22}^{T}&h_{12}^{T}&I\end{pmatrix}

(7)

Suppose
{{\cal A}}_{P^{\prime}P"}=(\alpha_{P^{\prime}},\beta_{P"})+{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{P^{\prime}P"}.

Then we can write
{{\cal A}}_{P^{\prime}P"}
as the solution space of the equations

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}i_{P_{1}"}\\
v_{P_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&r_{22}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v_{P_{1}^{\prime}}\\
i_{P_{2}"}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}s_{P_{1}"}\\
s_{P_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix},

(8)

where

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}s_{P_{1}"}\\
s_{P_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}=-\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&r_{22}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\alpha_{P_{1}^{\prime}}\\
\beta_{P_{2}"}\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}\beta_{P_{1}"}\\
\alpha_{P_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix},

(9)

Algorithm I

Input: A multiport
{\cal N}_{P}:\equiv({\cal G}_{SP},{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}),

where

{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is
composed
entirely of voltage sources, current sources, CCVS,VCCS,CCCS,VCVS
and resistors
and satisfies sufficiency conditions of

Theorem
68
.

Output: A hybrid representation for the port behaviour
{{\cal A}}_{P^{\prime}P"}
of
{\cal N}_{P}.

Step 1. Construct a
forest
t
of
{\cal G}_{SP}
that contains
Z_{1}\uplus Y_{1}\uplus E
and does not
intersect

Z_{2}\uplus Y_{2}\uplus J.

Step 2. Let

P_{1}:\equiv t\cap P,P_{2}:\equiv P-t.

The set of columns
P_{1}^{\prime}\uplus P_{2}"
is a column base of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}_{P^{\prime}P"}.

Step 3. Set all internal sources to zero (i.e., the device characteristic
is the

vector space associate of the original device characteristic).

For each element
e_{m}\in P_{1},
in turn, set
v_{e_{m}^{\prime}}=1,v_{e_{j}^{\prime}}=0,e_{j}\in P_{1},j\neq m,i_{P_{2}"}=0_{P_{2}"}
and

for each
e_{r}\in P_{2}
in turn, set
i_{e_{r}"}=1,i_{e_{j}"}=0,e_{j}\in P_{2},j\neq r,v_{P_{1}^{\prime}}=0_{P_{1}^{\prime}}
and compute

the corresponding vector

\begin{pmatrix}i_{P_{1}"}\\
v_{P_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

These yield the
m^{th}
and
(|P_{1}|+r)^{th}
columns respectively

of the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}&h_{12}\\
h_{21}&r_{22}\end{pmatrix}.

Step 4. Keep the internal sources active (i.e., the device characteristic

remains the original device characteristic of the multiport),
set
v_{P_{1}^{\prime}}=0_{P_{1}^{\prime}},i_{P_{2}"}=0_{P_{2}"}
and

solve the multiport
to compute the corresponding vector

\begin{pmatrix}i_{P_{1}"}\\
v_{P_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

This yields
\begin{pmatrix}s_{P_{1}"}\\
s_{P_{2}^{\prime}}\end{pmatrix}.

STOP

Algorithm
I

is justified by part 3 of Theorem
68
:
if
t
is a forest of
{\cal G}_{SP}
that contains
Z_{1}\uplus Y_{1}\uplus E
and does not
intersect
Z_{2}\uplus Y_{2}\uplus J,
and
\bar{t}:\equiv S\uplus P-t,

then
t^{\prime}\uplus\bar{t}"
is a column base of
\hat{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}

:\equiv(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}}\oplus(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{SP}))_{S"P"},
such that
(t^{\prime}\uplus\bar{t}")\cap(P^{\prime}\uplus P")=P_{1}^{\prime}\uplus P_{2}"

is a column base of

\hat{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}.

7.3
Generalized multiports based on Dirac structures

In this subsection we examine the case where the device characteristic
contains gyrators and ideal transformers. There are three ways in which we can
approach this problem. First, we could model these devices in terms of controlled sources. If we take the parameters of these controlled sources
to be algebraically indepedent over
{\mathbb{Q}},
Theorem
68
would be applicable. But this would only be an approximate model
of gyrators and ideal transformers. Since the approximation can be made arbitrarily
accurate, this may be adequate for practical purposes. A second approach would be to take the parameters of the gyrators
and ideal transformers to be algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}}.

This problem has been tackled with some success in the case of electrical
networks with no ports (see
[
31
]
for a detailed
discussion). However, the algorithm for testing whether the network
has a unique solution in this case is very cumbersome in comparison
with the case where we have only resistors and controlled and independent
sources.
Further, there are important situations
where the gyrators and transformers are used to impose constraints analogous to
topological ones. Here the assumption of algebraic independence is no
longer valid. We use below an approach with uses conventional circuit simulators
for computing the port behaviour.
In brief, we proceed as follows.
Let

\hat{{\cal N}}_{P}:\equiv({\cal G}_{\hat{S}P},{\cal D}_{S_{1}^{\prime}S_{1}"}\oplus{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}),

where
\hat{S}=S_{1}\uplus S,

{\cal D}_{S_{1}^{\prime}S_{1}"}
is a Dirac structure (i.e., satisfies

({\cal D}_{S_{1}^{\prime}S_{1}"})_{S_{1}"S_{1}^{\prime}}={\cal D}_{S_{1}^{\prime}S_{1}"}^{\perp}
) and
{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is composed
of resistors, independent and controlled sources as in Theorem
68
.
By Theorem
25
, we know that
\hat{{\cal N}}_{P}
is rigid iff the multiport

{{\cal N}}_{P}:\equiv({\cal G}_{\hat{S}P},{\cal D}_{S_{1}^{\prime}S_{1}"})

and the generalized multiport
{\cal N}^{g}_{P}:\equiv({\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"},{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"})

are rigid, where
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}:\equiv[(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{v}({\cal G}_{\hat{S}P}))_{\hat{S}^{\prime}P^{\prime}},(\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}^{i}({\cal G}_{\hat{S}P}))_{\hat{S}"P"}]\leftrightarrow{\cal D}_{S_{1}^{\prime}S_{1}"}

is a Dirac structure (Corollary
12
).
We test for rigidity of
{{\cal N}}_{P}
using a
conventional simulator to compute its port behaviour
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S_{1}^{\prime}P^{\prime}S_{1}"P"}.

The following theorem states, analogous to Theorem
68
,
that if certain columns of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}
can be included in column
bases and cobases of appropriate minors of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"},
then the generalized multiport
{\cal N}^{g}_{P}:\equiv({\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"},{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"})
is rigid.

Theorem 69
.

Let
{\cal N}^{g}_{P}:\equiv({\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"},{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}),
be a generalized multiport whose device characteristic

{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}
is composed
entirely of voltage sources, current sources, CCVS,VCCS,CCCS,VCVS and resistors as in Equations
4
,
5
,
6
.
Let
Y_{1}:\equiv\tilde{Y}_{1}\uplus\hat{Y}_{1},Y_{2}:\equiv\tilde{Y}_{2}\uplus\hat{Y}_{2},Z_{1}:\equiv\tilde{Z}_{1}\uplus\hat{Z}_{1},Z_{2}:\equiv\tilde{Z}_{2}\uplus\hat{Z}_{2}.

Let the values of resistors
and controlled source parameters be algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}}.

Further, let
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}
be Dirac.

1.
Necessity for multiport being rigid
Let
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}
be the vector space translate of
{\cal A}_{S^{\prime}S"}.

Then
\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}_{S^{\prime}S"}

has full sum property with

{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\circ S^{\prime}S"
and zero intersection property with

{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\times S^{\prime}S",

only if
the sets of columns
Y^{\prime}_{1}\uplus E^{\prime},Y"_{2}\uplus J",
of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"},
are independent.

2.
Sufficiency for multiport being rigid
The generalized multiport
{\cal N}^{g}_{P}
is rigid

a) if
the set of columns
Z^{\prime}_{1}\uplus Y^{\prime}_{1}\uplus E^{\prime},
can be included in a column base of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\circ S^{\prime}P^{\prime}
and the set of columns

Z^{\prime}_{2}\uplus Y^{\prime}_{2}\uplus J"
can be included in a column cobase of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\circ S^{\prime}P^{\prime},
i.e.,
Z"_{2}\uplus Y"_{2}\uplus J"
is an independent set of columns
of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\times S"P".

or

b) if the set of columns
Z"_{2}\uplus Y"_{2}\uplus J",
can be included in a column base of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\circ S"P"
and the set of columns

Z"_{1}\uplus Y"_{1}\uplus E"
can be included in a column cobase of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\circ S"P",
i.e.,
Z^{\prime}_{1}\uplus Y^{\prime}_{1}\uplus E^{\prime}
is an independent set of columns
of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"}\times S^{\prime}P^{\prime}.

3.
Existence of hybrid representation

Let the above conditions of 2(a) or 2(b) be satisfied.
Then there exists a
column base
b_{1}
of
{\mbox{$\mathcal{V}$}}^{1}_{S^{\prime}P^{\prime}S"P"},
that contains
Z^{\prime}_{1}\uplus Y^{\prime}_{1}\uplus E^{\prime}\uplus Z"_{2}\uplus Y"_{2}\uplus J".

For such a column base, the port behaviour of
{\cal N}^{g}_{P}

has a hybrid representation
with
(b_{1}\cap P^{\prime})\uplus(b_{1}\cap P")
as a column base for the vector space
translate of the behaviour.

Remark 10
.

We note that Theorem
69
requires testing of independence of certain columns of the port behaviour of
{\cal N}_{SP}:\equiv({\cal G}_{S_{1}SP},D_{S_{1}^{\prime}S_{1}"})

Usually the size of
S\uplus P
would be much greater than that of
S_{1}.

Computing the port behaviour of
{\cal N}_{SP}
directly would be expensive.
However, the ports
S\uplus P
would have redundancies in
{\cal N}_{SP}.

We show in
D
, that in such cases we can compute the port behaviour of a multiport in which the ports are minimized and the independence can be reduced to testing that of a reduced set of columns in the minimized port behaviour. Further, it is shown there that the minimized number of ports
is not greater than
|S_{1}|.

8
Conclusions and future work

We have introduced the notion of rigidity for linear multiports as
the counterpart of uniqueness of solution for linear networks
and shown its utility in understanding the behaviour of linear networks
composed of multiports.

We have shown that there is an analogous notion of rigidity for pairs of matroids and that its properties mimic those for multiports.
We have exploited this analogy for giving simple and very efficient topological tests
for rigidity of electrical multiports composed of nonzero resistors, independent and
controlled voltage and independent and
controlled current sources assuming that the parameters of the devices
are algebraically independent over
{\mathbb{Q}}.

We mention below a few lines of future research.

Gyrators and ideal transformers are important electrical components
and also means of imposing conditions similar to topological
ones.
In this paper, we have handled
the case where the algebraic independence condition
is not valid.
If one assumes algebraic independence over
{\mathbb{Q}}

for their parameters, one can develop purely matroidal tests for
rigidity of multiports containing them, analogous to the theory for
uniqueness of solution of networks containing them
[
31
,
12
]
.

Orthogonality is an important property in computational linear algebra.
Rigidity is a weaker form of complementary orthogonality (Subsection
5.3
). It is the best that can be asked
for, when the two spaces model different physical entities
(such as topological and device characteristic).
Similarly for matroids, rigidity can be regarded as a weaker form of duality (Subsection
6.4
).
It may be interesting to explore this aspect of rigidity.

Both matched composition and the implicit duality theorem (Theorems
11
,
50
) hold for polymatroid rank functions
[
33
]
. It appears worthwhile
to explore the relevance of the notion of rigidity for such functions.

Appendix A
Rigidity of matroid pairs: proofs

Proof.

(
Proof of Lemma
54
)

1. We have
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B}),
so that
|B|=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})
iff

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}^{1}_{B}\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}^{2}_{B})=0.

2. By Theorem
48
, we have

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times A)+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times C)+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)-r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B).

Now
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)-r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B).

Therefore, using Theorems
47
,
43
, we have

r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})

=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times A)+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times C)+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B)+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)-r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)-r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B)

=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})-r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B)-r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B).
Therefore,

we have
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})=r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB})+r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})-|B|,
if

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\circ B\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\circ B=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{B}
and

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB}\times B\wedge\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC}\times B={\mathbf{0}}_{B},

i.e., if
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{AB},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{BC})
is rigid.

∎

We need the following lemma for the proof of the result on rigidity of
pairs of matroids which are derived through the ‘
\leftrightarrow
’ operation
from a rigid pair of matroids.

Lemma 70
.

1.

[\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})]\circ V=\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ V\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V};

2.

[\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})]\times T=[(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\times T]\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T};

3.

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\circ T.

Proof.

Let us denote
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})
by
\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}.

1.
We need to show
(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})\circ V=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ V\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}).

We have
(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})\circ V=\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\circ V\vee(({\mathbf{0}}_{V}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})\circ V)=\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\circ V=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\circ V\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ V\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}).

2.
We need to show that
(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})\times T=(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\times T)\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}.

Let
\hat{b}_{T}
be a base of
(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\times T)\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}.

Then there exist bases
b^{1}_{TV},b^{2}_{T}
of
\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T},

respectively such that
\hat{b}_{T}=(b^{1}_{TV}\cap T)\cup b^{2}_{T}

and

b^{1}_{TV}\cap V
is a base of
\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\circ V,

i.e.,
(b^{1}_{TV}\cup b^{2}_{T})\cap V
is a base of
(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})\circ V.

It follows that
(b^{1}_{TV}\cup b^{2}_{T})\cap T=\hat{b}_{T}
is independent in
(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})\times T.

Let
\hat{b}_{T}
be a base of
(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})\times T.

Then there exist bases

b^{1}_{TV},b^{2}_{T}
of
\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T},
respectively such
that
(b^{1}_{TV}\cap T)\cup b^{2}_{T}=\hat{b}_{T},

and
(b^{1}_{TV}\cup b^{2}_{T})\cap V=b^{1}_{TV}\cap V
is a base of

(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T})\circ V=\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\circ V.

It follows that
(b^{1}_{TV}\cap T)
is independent in

\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\times T
and
(b^{1}_{TV}\cap T)\cup b^{2}_{T}=\hat{b}_{T},
is independent in
(\hat{\mbox{$\cal M$}}_{TV}\times T)\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}.

3.
\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\times T=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\circ TV\times T

=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\times WT\circ T
(using part 1 of Theorem
43
)

=(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\circ T.

∎

Proof.

(
Proof of Theorem
55
)

We have
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{TV}
iff

r[(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})]

=r[((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}))\circ V]+r[((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}))\times T]=|{T\uplus V}|,

using Theorem
43
.
Since
r(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{X})\leq|X|,

we have
(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{TV}
iff

((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}))\circ V=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{V},

and

((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}))\times T=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{T},

i.e., iff

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ V\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{V},

and

((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\times T)\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{T},

using part 1 and 2 of Lemma
70
,
i.e., iff

\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ V\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{V},

and

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\circ TV\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}=((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\circ T)\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{T},

using Theorem
49
, using part 3 of Lemma
70
.

Thus the full sum property holds for
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})\}
iff it holds for
\{\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV},\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}\}
and for

\{(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}),\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\}.

We will now use Theorems
42
,
47
,
43
and the Implicit
Duality Theorem (Theorem
50
) and get the second half
of the property of rigidity that is required to complete the proof.

From the above argument, we have

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}^{*}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}^{*}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}^{*})=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{TV},

iff

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}^{*}\circ V)\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}^{*}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{V}
and

((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}^{*}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}^{*})\circ T)\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}^{*}=\mbox{$\cal F$}_{T},

i.e.,
((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}^{*}\circ TV)\vee(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}^{*}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}^{*}))^{*}=\mbox{$\cal F$}^{*}_{TV}

iff

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}^{*}\circ V\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}^{*})^{*}=\mbox{$\cal F$}^{*}_{V}
and

(((\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}^{*}\leftrightarrow\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V}^{*})\circ T)\vee\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}^{*})^{*}=\mbox{$\cal F$}^{*}_{T},

i.e., (using Theorems
42
,
47
)

(\mbox{$\cal M$}_{WTV}\times TV)\ \wedge\ (\mbox{$\cal M$}_{T}\oplus\mbox{$\cal M$}_{V})={\mathbf{0}}_{TV},

iff
